3.8       The Effects of SOX-Related Controls on the Exposition
          or Prevention of Fraud on Financial Statements and
          Listing of the Controls in Terms of Their Importance

The SOX Act became law in response to many fraudulent disclosures in financial
statements of some large American corporations. It was deemed necessary to
initiate and install some control mechanisms that could assist in uncovering and
preventing fraudulent disclosure in financial statements. In the ACFE (2008) report,
the effects of SOX-related control mechanisms in all financial statements to detect
fraud activities were analyzed. The report found that the presence of the precautions
taken to prevent and control fraud did not necessarily reduce the approximate loss
as a result of fraud in financial statements, but only hotlines were able to meet the
objective. In the same report, it was mentioned that companies which apply SOX-
related control were able to confront fraudulent practices in financial statements.
The report notes that the detection of fraudulent activities in financial statement
took longer in companies which have independent audit committees.
    Table 3.18 provides information on SOX-related control. It could be seen that
the table reveals some interesting findings. Except for hotlines, other controls are
found to have a positive effect on the prevention of fraud in financial statements.
Management Certification of F/S has revealed some negative effects with a rate of
169.2%. In companies where no such control exists, the loss incurred was about
$1,300,000, while the figure for companies with no such control in place was
$3,500,000. Furthermore, in Table 3.18, Management Certification of F/S has
shown that the companies using this control tool detect frauds 5 months later than
the companies without this control tool. Another important control tool in the table
is the independent audit committee. Table 3.18 shows that in companies with the
independent audit committee, the approximate loss was $7,000,000, while that in
companies without the committee was $1,500,000, and the rate of loss was

the period for the companies with an independent audit committee was about
36 months. Its effect in uncovering financial statements fraud was 74%. The
effect of management review of internal control was set to be 50%. Hotlines were
found to be the only control tool with a positive effect on approximate losses in
SOX-related control of fraud in financial statements. The companies with hotlines
have approximate loss of $2,000,000, while the companies without them lost
$2,500,000. Additionally, in Table 3.18, it was seen that companies with hotlines
uncover frauds 4 months earlier than the companies without them.
   Another important subject seen in the ACFE (2008) report was the order of
control in terms of their importance in uncovering or preventing fraud. According
to the ACFEs report, in asset misappropriations (employee’s frauds), corruption,
and financial statement fraud, the more important control mechanisms are shown as
follows, in Table 3.19.
   In the detection of fraud in terms of the misuse of company’s resources (inter-
nally committed fraud—employees fraud), the most effective method comes in via
the system of internal audit. This is followed by surprise audits and the management
examination of the internal control; hotlines were in the fifth position. The effec-
tiveness of these methods was ranked in the following order: in first place was
internal audit, in the second place was hotlines, and this was followed by surprise
audit. Hotline was found to be more effective in uncovering corruption than
employees’ frauds. Like the independent audit of the financial statements, control
was primarily aimed at determining the financial statement fraud. Despite the
rewards for whistleblowers which was thought to be less effective in uncovering
and preventing the financial statements’ fraud, it is occurring in the first place and
this is followed by internal audit and hotlines. This situation overlaps with the data
in Table 3.18, on SOX-related control in financial table statements.

    Conclusion
    As a company becomes more successful in financial terms, the number of
    stakeholders wanting to use its financial statements in making a series of
    decisions increases. It is, therefore, important that the information contained in
    such financial statements is accurate and reliable. The list of these stakeholders is
    long. Some of them are managers, collaborative partners, employees, investors,
    credit institutions, suppliers, customers, tax authority, and consumers. The
    information passed on to them must be reliable to assist them in making
    informed decisions.
       Corporate financial scandals emanating from fraudulent financial reporting
    and other irregularities are not only a disease of the developed world, but are also
    a phenomenon of the less developed parts of the world, albeit not as widely
    publicized as those in the well-developed parts of the world. The occurrence of
    these financial scandals has resulted in questioning the effectiveness of the
    regulations and operations designed to uncover and prevent fraud. According
    to the limited data available, the hotlines put in operation after the SOX Law
    appear to have some desirable effects with public companies, private, and
    nonprofit organizations in order to uncover and prevent the incidence of fraud.
    Together with hotlines, surprise audits, management review of internal control,
    the rewards for whistleblowers, mandatory job rotation/vacations, and internal
    audits are also effective in uncovering and preventing such crime.
       In some studies carried out on employees’ frauds, corruption, and fraudulent
    financial reporting, which are generally grouped as internally perpetrated fraud
    and dealt with in the reports used in this study, a distinct decrease in the
    incidence of fraud was not observed. This was noticeable in fraud related to
    financial statements, which was the starting point of most corporate scandals
    experienced in different parts of the world. In the ACFE report of 2008, apart
    from hotline, which is one of the SOX-related control systems in financial
    statements fraud, other types of control that could be used are ineffective in
62                                                                                          N. Çıtak


     reducing other categories of fraud. We can only hope that other actions taken by
     governments and law-enforcing agencies would serve as a deterrent in
     preventing this global disease for the sake of everyone.

Forensic Accounting for Financial
Malpractices in Developing Countries: An                                                  4
Analytical Review of the State of the Art

Abubakar S. Kasum




4.1       Introduction

Financial irregularity is a problem of global concern. It is a major concern to
developing nations of the world. It is so endemic that fraud and corruption are
gradually becoming normal way of life in third world countries. Financial
irregularities are so common that almost no individual can wash his or her hands
clean of it. Although it is witnessed in both the public and the private sector
organizations, the prevalence is higher in the public sector of developing
economies. Public funds are in many instances misused by the political class of
these countries. Experience in Nigeria shows that financial malpractice incidents
are attributable to office holders from the presidential to the ward level (ward is the
smallest unit of government usually having representation at the local government).
No money is entirely free; every amount of money has its legal use, and conse-
quently, misuse of any amount would have some negative effects, on some other
aspects of life in the economy, in particular the legally intended area. The ultimate
effect of these can be felt by the organization or a whole nation. If the effect is not
felt directly, it may have some indirect effect on facilities and infrastructure that
were intended to be positively affected.
    Unless it is impossible, individuals or establishments adversely affected by the
fraudulent or corrupt practices might want to seek redress. The individual, corpo-
rate body, and interested government organs would consequently like to take
actions toward seeking redress using divergent institutions like the police and the
law court. Whatever an investigator wants to do will not be complete if the extent to
which the affected person is unquantifiable. This and other pecuniary matters are
the areas where the service of an expert, the “forensic accountant,” has been
engaged for a very long time in some developed countries and probably, recently,
extending to developing nations. It is not that financial misappropriation is


A.S. Kasum
Department of Accounting and Finance, University of Ilorin, Nigeria

K. Çalıyurt and S.O. Idowu (eds.), Emerging Fraud,                                 65
DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-20826-3_4, # Springer-Verlag Berlin Heidelberg 2012
66                                                                         A.S. Kasum


completely eradicated in the developed countries of the world. It occurs even in
sophisticated forms. So much that Grazoli et al. (2006) confirmed that detection by
looking for red flags and various other clues of deceit has become of the question
effectiveness for fraud. It is, however, too rampant and not adequately tackled in the
developing countries, and perpetrators get away, almost always, with the act. This is
to say that the nefarious acts are not uncovered, investigated, prosecuted, and
adequately punished. Consequently, many of those involved are left free and
hence there is no deterrent for future acts. This study provides an overview of
forensic accounting, as a tool in the fight against financial crimes that have
impacted seriously and negatively on the economies of the third world nations.


4.2      Forensic Accounting

Joshi (2003) ascribed the origination of forensic accounting to Kutilya, the first
economist to openly recognize the need for the forensic accountant. He mentioned
40 ways of embezzlement centuries ago. He, however, stated that the term “forensic
accounting” was coined by Peloubet in 1946. Crumbley (2001) wrote on the same
issue when he stated that a form of forensic accounting can be traced back to an
1817 court decision, and also that a “young Scottish accountant issued a circular
advertising his expertise in arbitration support in 1824” but that Peloubet was
probably the first to publish the phrase forensic accounting. Investigation of fraud
and corruption is confirmed thus, not to be new, even in Nigeria, the country of this
author. It is only gaining prominence because of the growing wave of the crime
under the seemingly new nomenclature of the last 5 years (Coenen 2005).
   Forensic accounting, which is also called investigative accounting or fraud audit,
is a merger of forensic science and accounting. Forensic science according to
Crumbley (2003) “may be defined as application of the laws of nature to the laws
of man” and refers to forensic scientists as examiners and interpreters of evidence
and facts in legal cases and offers experts’ opinions regarding their findings in the
court of law. The science in question here is accounting science, meaning that the
examination and interpretation will be of economic information.
   Joshi (2003) defined forensic accounting as the “application of specialized
knowledge and specific skill to stumble up on the evidence of economic
transactions.” Zysman (2001) puts forensic accounting as the integration of
accounting, auditing, and investigative skills. Simply put, forensic accounting is
accounting that is suitable for legal review, offering the highest level of assurance
and including the now generally accepted connotation of having been arrived at in a
scientific fashion (Crumbley 2006).
   Coenen (2005) stated that forensic accounting involves the application of
accounting concepts and techniques to legal problems. It demands reporting
where the accountability of the fraud is established, and the report is considered
as evidence in the court of law or in the administrative proceeding (Joshi). It
provides an accounting analysis that is suitable to the court, which will form the
basis of discussion, debate, and ultimately dispute resolution (Zysman 2001).
4   Forensic Accounting for Financial Malpractices in Developing Countries                           67


   This means that forensic accounting is a field of specialization that has to do with
the provision of information that is meant to be used as evidence, especially for
legal purposes. The persons practicing in this field (i.e., forensic accountants)
investigate and document financial fraud and white-collar crimes such as embez-
zlement and investigate allegations of fraud; estimate losses, damages, and assets;
and analyze complex financial transactions. They provide these services for corpo-
ration, attorneys, criminal investigators, and the Government (Coenen 2005). Their
engagements are usually geared toward finding where the money went, how it got
there, and who was responsible. They are trained to look beyond the numbers and
deal with business reality of the situation (Zysman 2001).
    Investigative and forensic accounting engagements are those that:- require the application
    of professional accounting skills, investigative skills, and an investigative mindset; and -
    involve disputes or anticipated disputes, or where there are risks, concerns or allegations of
    fraud or other illegal or unethical conduct (Canadian Institute of Chartered Accountant
    2006).



4.3        Composition of Forensic Accounting

Forensic accounting encompasses both litigation support and investigative account-
ing. Litigation support provides assistance of all nature in a matter involving
existing or pending litigation. It deals primarily with issues related to the quantifi-
cation of economic damages, while investigative accounting is associated with the
investigation of criminal matters (Zysman 2001).
   Under litigation support, forensic accountants assist in the following:
• Obtaining documentary evidence to support or refute a claim.
• Reviewing of the relevant documentation to form an initial assessment of the
   case in an identified area of loss.
• Examining for discovering, including, the formulation of the act regarding the
   financial residence.
• Attending the examination for discovery to review the testimony, assist with
   understanding the financial issues, and formulate additional questions to be asked.
   Investigative accountants, on the other hand, perform the following:
• Review factual situation and provide suggestions regarding possible courses of
   action
• Assist with the protection and recovery of assets
• Coordinate and cooperate with other experts (Zysman 2001)


4.4        Forensic Accounting Engagement

Coenen (2005) identifies the following as areas of specialty in forensic accounting:
• Investigating corporate fraud
• Litigation services
• Business valuation
• Computer forensic
68                                                                        A.S. Kasum


  However, Zysman (2001) in a more elaborate form captured the assignments
undertaken by forensic accountants as including the following:
• Criminal investigations, which are usually on behalf of the police with the aim of
  presenting evidence in a professional and concise manner.
• Shareholder and partnership disputes that involve analysis of numerous years of
  financial records for valuation and qualification of the issue in dispute.
• Personal injury claims, where, for example, economic losses have been suffered
  following a motor accident or wrongful dismissal by an employer. They help
  quantify these losses.
• Business interruption and other types of insurance claims. These assignments
  involve a detailed review of the policy to investigate coverage issues and the
  appropriate methods of calculating the loss.
• Business/employee fraud investigations, which can involve fraud tracing, asset
  identification and recovery, forensic intelligence gathering, and due diligence
  review.
• Matrimonial dispute involving the tracing, locating, and evaluation of assets.
• Business economic losses, where contract disputes, construction claims, expro-
  priation, product liability claims, and trademark problems are the issues.
• Professional negligence, to ascertain the bridge and quantify the loss involved.
• Mediation and arbitration, as a form of alternative dispute resolution.




4.5     Approach to Work

Zysman (2001) outlined the following steps in executing forensic engagements:
– Meet with the client to obtain an understanding of the important facts, players,
  and issues at hand
– Perform a conflict check as soon as the relevant parties are established
– Perform an initial investigation to allow subsequent planning to be based on a
  more complete understanding of the issues
– Develop an action plan that takes into account the knowledge gained by meeting
  with the client and carrying out the initial investigation, and set out the
  objectives to be achieved and the methodology to be utilized to accomplish them
– Obtain the relevant evidence (this may involve locating documents, economic
  information, assets, a person or company, another expert, or proof of the
  occurrence of an event)
– Perform the analysis (this may involve calculating economic damages,
  summarizing a large number of transactions, performing a tracing of assets,
  performing present value calculations utilizing appropriate discount rates,
  performing a regression or sensitivity analysis, or utilizing a computerized
  application such as spreadsheet, database, or computer model, and utilizing
  charts and graphics to explain the analysis)
– Prepare the report
4   Forensic Accounting for Financial Malpractices in Developing Countries          69


   Often a report will be prepared which may include sections on the nature of the
assignment, scope of the investigation, approach utilized, limitation of scope, and
findings and/or opinions. The report will include schedules and graphics necessary
to support or explain the event properly. Joshi (2003) stated that the job demands
reporting where the accountability of the fraud is established, and the report is
considered as evidence in the court of law or in the administrative proceeding. The
Canadian Institute of Chartered Accountant recommends communication of work
in the form of the following:
– Written, oral, or visual
– Draft, interim or final
– Points form or narrative
– Formal, affidavit or letter



4.6       Organization of the Forensic Accounting Profession

Forensic accounting is an offshoot of the general professional accounting and is
affiliated to professional accounting bodies. For the purpose of practice, individual
firms of general accounting practitioners departmentalize and train specialists in
investigative accounting within their general practice firm. For example, the Cana-
dian Institute of Chartered Accountants established the Alliance for excellence in
investigative and forensic accounting in 1998. Crumbley (2003) also stated that
forensic accountants are typically CPA/CAs who specialize in those types of
engagements where there is a need for such evidence. In the words of Crumbley
(2006), the forensic accountant is a certified public accountant who performs an
orderly analysis in an attempt to obtain the truth on which to form an expert
opinion. In firm practice, Crumbley (2006) stated that there are between 380 and
500 forensic accountants in the firm of PricewaterhouseCoopers.
    On the other side, separate bodies are also springing up for forensic accountants,
and professional bodies specializing in same discipline are being established.
Examples are the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners, National Association
of Forensic Accountants, America College of Forensic Examiners, Certified Fraud
Deterrence, Certified Forensic Financial Analyst, and Forensic CPA Society
(Crumbley 2006). Some specialist practice firms include Zymas Forensic Account-
ing Incorporated, Kessler International, Sequence Incorporated, Gottschalk Foren-
sic Accounting and Valuation Inc., LBC International Investigative Accounting
Inc., etc.
    The practice is just gaining popularity in Nigeria, one of the largest economies in
Black Africa. As such, there can only be instances of professionals engaging in
investigative activities, and there can be knowledgeable and experienced person
in investigative accounting. The kind of organizations mentioned above which are
common in Canada and the USA is being recently established in Nigeria.
70                                                                             A.S. Kasum


4.7      Financial Malpractices

Williams (2005) defines corrupt practice as any immoral, illegal, and unethical act
which includes cheating, lies, fraud, etc. Khan (2005) puts corruption as the misuse
of resources entrusted in ones’ power for private benefit. The forms, he said, include
bribes, cronyism and nepotism, political donations, kickbacks and artificial pricing,
and fraud of all kinds. Fraud also has been classified into three categories, which
include asset misappropriation, corruption, and fraudulent statements. They involve
theft or misuse of assets, improper use of influence in a transaction for own benefit,
and falsification of financial statement, respectively (O’Keefe et al. 2001).
   Ribadu (2004) identified the obstacle to prosecution of corruption to include
failure of law enforcement agencies, constitutional constraints, attitude of defense
lawyers, inadequacy of existing procedural laws, congestion and slow pace of court
proceedings, jurisdiction problem, and cost of investigation and prosecution.
   In the public sector, “total revenue from oil and gas will never be totally accurate
because in our governing situation there is plenty of money. A clique of politicians
and impunity reigns allowing corruption to thrive”. . .. big firms collude with those
who are responsible for governance, i.e., those. . .who have the power to collect the
revenue derived from the exploitation and exportation (Williams 2005).
   Table 4.1 shows some of the high-profile embezzlements by some developing
nations’ leaders during the last 40 years.
   The effects of the above scenario are reflected in many standard of living indices
of the countries concerned, as shown in the GDP per capita and others in the table
below.

and school enrolment of developing countries shown in the table are not

comparable with those of developed economies like the USA, Japan, and the UK.
Where the developed nations are talking of GDP of forty thousand US Dollars
($40,000+) and above per head, developing countries are talking in the range of one
hundred to one thousand five hundred US Dollars ($100–$1,500). Life expectancy
of above 70 years is noted in the table for the developed nations, and 40s to early

developing countries in other indices shown in the table. What could be inferred
from the table is that resources that would have gone into improving people’s lives
are embezzled by their leaders and those around them. Going by the above, one
would agree, nonetheless, that financial malpractice is a menace that should be
battled, especially in public offices, in the interest of citizens.



4.8       Conditions Favorable to Corrupt Practices

According to Babalobi (2008), causes of corruption in Nigeria include:
– Weak government institution
– Poor pay incentives
72                                                                       A.S. Kasum


– Lack of openness and transparency in public service
– Absence of key anticorruption tools
– Ineffective political processes
– Culture and acceptance of corruption by the populace
– Absence of effective political financing
– Poverty
– Ethnic and religious difference
– Resources scramble
   The above-listed conditions could be said to be common in many third world
countries like Nigeria—this author’s home country. It is possible that his
experiences could have influenced his submission in this chapter.


     Conclusion
  Through our review, we are able to establish the nature and meaning of forensic
  accounting, what forensic accountants do, who are the practitioners in the field,
  and how they are organized. We have also studied the nature of corrupt practices
  in developing economies, the magnitude of some documented cases, and the
  negative impacts they have on citizens’ welfare. Based on the result of our
  library analysis, we conclude the following:
– Forensic accounting practice is neither new internationally nor new in develop-
  ing countries.
– Financial malpractices taking place in developing countries are alarming and are
  not only crumbling the economy, but also affecting the image of these countries.
– The services of the experts (forensic accountant) are required in developing
  countries, for fight against fraud and corruption cases.
– Their services are required in the public sector organizations of developing
  countries.
     Adefila et al. (2005) stated that fraud and corrupt practices are globally
  endemic. Tackling it, therefore, becomes also a global challenge. A good
  forensic structure can help alleviate developing countries’ plight. In line with
  the above, the following are suggested:
– The Institute of Chartered Accountant or Auditors in the developing countries
  should encourage formalization and specialization in the field of forensic
  accounting.
– The governments should develop interest in forensic accounting and accountants
  for monitoring and investigating suspected and confirmed corruption cases.
– Practicing accountants in the countries concerned should rise up to the chal-
  lenge, work toward specialization, and possibly establish firms for forensic
  accounting practice only.
– Academia should emphasize skill development in the field of forensic
  accounting.

Growth (GDP Rate)

Alexandru Trifu




5.1      Introduction

The underground economy, black market, or “shadow” economy is considered a
market consisting of all kinds of commerce, on which applicable taxes and/or
regulations, and procedures of trade are being avoided. In modern societies, the
underground economy covers a vast area of activities. It is generally smallest in
countries where economic development and freedom are the greatest, and become
progressively larger in areas where corruption, regulations imposed, or legal
monopolies restrict legitimate economic activity.
   It is considered an informal economy and includes not only illegal activities
(which are the order of the day in this market), but also legal ones, regarding
merchandises and services, which incomes are undeclared in order to avoid the
payment of applicable taxation. The precise understanding lies on the specific
mechanism of taxation and regulations implemented in each country considered.
   The shadow economy needs a perfect delineation or understanding, with a view
to achieving pertinent analyses, and forecasts actions that are to be followed by the
removal of some activities from this sphere and their transfer within a real econ-
omy. The whole mechanism, thus conceived, has been based on the use and
implementation of the general social-economic development policies led by the
government as a kind of universal panacea of equally solving the problems related
to the shadow economy.
   It is believed that the shadow economy is mainly subsistence economy where
black market labor reaches approximately half of this kind of economy. As we
intend to point out in what follows, the main cause of a shadow economy is the
incidence of high level taxation, which results in the massive tax evasion
phenomenon.

Gheorghe Zane University, Jassy, Romania

5.2       Shadow Economy, Corruption, Economic Infrastructure,
          and Romania

5.2.1     Shadow Economy

For Romania, more than two thirds (67%) of the shadow economy are found in the
formal sector of the economy, that is, in the sector represented by the legal bodies
organized as trading companies, quasi-companies, and public institutions. And, as
we speak about exports, respectively imports, when we refer to the adhesion to an
economic block formed of several states, such as the European Union (EU), the
weakly developed states undergoing the transition process contribute in different
percentages to the shadow economy of the community. Romania, in its turn, takes
part in this process with one-third (33%) of its gross domestic product (GDP) and a
quarter of its manpower.
   Speaking at the level of the fiscal stress (the fiscal degree), that is, of the
determining vector in defalcating a more or less important part of the economic
activity, we feel the need of a comparison, an important one in our opinion, between
the EU, the different OECD member states, and Romania. A comparison drawn up
in 2004, the year of the most important EU expansion “wave,” is as follows:

OECD                                                                           37.7%
EU                                                                             42.4%
Romania                                                                        29.6%
Source: Revenue Statistics, OECD, 2004
    In the case of Romania, this author’s country, the lower taxation degree against
the average of certain OECD, or UE member states, is explained for that date by the
narrow taxation scale (like nowadays, by neglecting the evidence of Laffer’s curve
teachings), as well as by a consequence of the existing fiscal evasion phenomenon.
    Since we cannot avoid mentioning the curve highlighted by the Californian
Professor Arthur Laffer, there is, in its frame, a situation that interests us, the
minimum taxation rate, that is, the rate matching not only minimal but also
efficient red tape so that the whole state mechanism can function.
    While the fiscal tax may be submitted to quantification, the intensity of the
regulations represented by the assembly of disposition, procedures, and operation
that is applied to taxpayers to get the working license, or to its normal working,
constitutes the major cause of the trend toward a shadow economy.
    This type of economy is a relevant presence, even if in a discrete manner, in all
states, regardless of their socioeconomic development and the political system that
governs them. Still, we can make significant differences in this respect. Thus, while
the transitional countries are affected, in the first place, by fiscal fraud which
consists of the main black market labor exporters, the developed countries focus
on fighting against criminal activities on the role.
5   The Double-Way İnterrelationship Between the Shadow Economy                   77


5.2.2    Corruption

Corruption constitutes, however, the determining factor in the existence of shadow
economy. A large amount of the facilities obtained as a consequence of the
corruptive act return to the shadow economy, while the rest by means of the
well-known money-laundering procedure, that is, recycled, enter the real economy,
a fact which is especially noticed when talking about fiscal paradise. All starts from
the way the law is observed by the civil servants who, in exchange for “small gifts,”
either cancel, postpone, or spread out certain payments, or grant fiscal facilities to
help the enterprising individual reach his/her goal, more or less honestly.
    Corruption is an economic activity, and we can even talk about the economics
of corruption, since one starts with rather limited resources, undergoes specific
operational processes, benefits of special “financing”, has special action and affir-
mation possibilities and while his/her final goal is not only satisfying different
interests, but mainly achieving and maximizing the profit. Moreover, corruption is
an extremely real, national, not submitted to the hazard and which has strong
historical, economic, social and political determinations.
    We can speak, in this case, as a significant characterization, of interrelations
between more powerful psychological elements (subjectively, specific to each
person) and the economic-social realities to deal with.
    The consequences of the corruptive process are to be noticed in the replacement
of resource distribution by means of the mechanisms that are specific for the free,
competitive market with the “shadow economy,” which is only possible because of
the general situation in the economy, to the interfering degree of the state, to the
visible political and ethical factors. The corruptive mechanism is very simple and
manifests by the participants’ desire to earn more, desire whose intensity increases
as the differences between the incomes of the corrupt ones (civil servants) and those
of the corruptors are significant, a decisive role being held to the moral environment
of that society.
    One of the indicators that may offer us an approximately true-to-life image over
the size of the global corruption phenomenon is the Corruption Perception Index
(CPI), a composite index, reached at following at least three surveys in the business
environment, reflecting the opinion of all the factors that make decisions
concerning the investment and trading activities. The index presents a variation
scale from 10 (inexistent corruption or almost inexistent) to 0 (the highest corrup-
tion level).
    This is how the data concerning this index would look based on the 2007 annual
report drawn up by Transparency International.
    The first place is held by Denmark with a score of 9.4, equal to that obtained by
Finland; Great Britain holds the 13th place with 8.4; France is in the 19th place with
7.3 points; Malta comes in the 33rd place with 5.8 points; Cyprus is in the 39th
place with 5.3; and the last places in the EU ranking are held by Bulgaria in the 64th
place with 4.1 points and Romania in the 70th place with 3.7 points.
    The Community’s average was 6.5, which means that Romania is far behind the
states that have a public, healthy, and balanced life. Taking into account the fact
78                                                                               A. Trifu


that in 2003 the CPI was of 2.8, we can notice that Romania has not been very
successful in reducing the size of this social shortcoming.
    However, according to the international available statistics from the end of the
last century, the transition group of countries has had a double percentage of the
shadow economy in terms of total official GDP than the group of the developed
countries within OECD (max. 30% than 16%). Compared with the developing
countries, the weight of the shadow economy is greater, with a maximum level
at between 45 and 50%.
    The problem of the weakly developed states, as well as those in transition, is the
fact that they cannot achieve the necessary requirements by themselves. This is
why it is necessary for the donating states (found on top of the CPI ranking) to assist
technically these other states by building the awareness of the public decision
makers and achieve the institutional integrity in the states facing a strong corruption
phenomenon.
    We also believe that for the transitional countries, which build and consolidate
their reforms and socioeconomic structures that are meant to be viable, are
influenced, also in what concerns the increase in the corruption level, by the
multinational companies, by those who act globally, and are interested to provide
hospitable hosts and who may grant as many economic advantages as possible so
that they might maximize the profits.
    As well as this, the multinational companies have in view the covering of the
most important segments of the transition states’ market, reaching in many cases
(including Romania) to hold if not a national monopoly, a real regional and zone
area monopoly. Not to mention the offshore areas that play a decisive role in
exploiting the resources, as well as in the money-laundering activity.
    Estimating the level of the shadow economy in the transitional countries is hard
to achieve, their size and projection into the real economy being rather unknown.
We can talk about a multidimensional phenomenon that encompasses more aspects
and causative factors than the ones presented in this paper.
    Since we were talking about the proportion that is difficult to forecast in terms of
the penetration between the real economy and the shadow economy, here is how
one of the most important macroeconomic indicators, the real economic growth,
and the underground (shadow economy) must exist in an understanding of the same
kind as between two opposite economic categories, while the efficient reduction
measures will have results only under the circumstances of advance no recession
periods. The tendency toward investment is inversely proportional to the level of
corruption in the analyzed host country.
    For Romania, this “practice,” this mechanism of the socioeconomic life, is old,
belonging to the Phanariot period, and preserved during the nineteenth and twenti-
eth centuries.
    This is why the measures to be implemented so as to reduce the weight of the
shadow economy on the echelons of corruption, of fiscal evasion, and of black labor
depend first of all not only on the government officials’ political will, but also on
that of the political class in general, to observe the law and nothing more. Watching
5   The Double-Way İnterrelationship Between the Shadow Economy                    79


all the tax dodgers and applying the corresponding penalties are also actions that
may prove efficient.
    We can also add the general measures of restricting the dimensions of the
corruption, so a kind of interruption from the source of the shadow economy:
– Punishing those that prove to be guilty of corrupt practices
– As much as possible, keeping the politicians away from the activities of the
    public administration
– Motivating payment with adequate and flexible rewarding systems
– Selecting and promoting the employees according to their performance (in fact,
    the last two measures have to be considered of maximum importance)
    However, we believe that knowing the economic theory and practice within the
developed economies with a very low level of corruption and with vigorous and
healthy public life, since this is how one can really put into practice the free market
mechanism, where a very important role reverts to the state, in its quality of third
important player in the economy.


5.2.3    Economic Infrastructure

However, we consider that the most significant liaison between shadow economy
(the precise degree or weight in the economy) and the amount of GDP is given by
the foundations of each economy of the world. None of these economies could
develop, increase the GDP rate, and, in nowadays conditions, overpass the financial
and economic crisis’ effects without a structure of the economy and society called
infrastructure, especially economic infrastructure, a complex and multidimen-
sional functional system.
   In the term infrastructure, the prefix infra in Latin means “under, beneath”;
therefore, the term means exactly those elements that sustain the whole construc-
tion of a national economy (the present case).
   In this way, this author has shaped the entire notional range of the concept of
economic infrastructure, i.e., of all basic aspects that make a region’s functioning
possible, for reaching the proposed purposes, first of all that of economic develop-
ment. The reach of the respective objectives of mezzo and macroeconomic nature
cannot be efficiently and expertly accomplished by private entrepreneurs, but the
same state interference is necessary for creating and moreover maintaining these
infrastructures which, through the elaborated regulations, might effectively provide
the national economy functioning under best conditions.
   The question righteously asked: what would be the optimum (desirable) level of
economic infrastructure which requires the best state intervention for the respec-
tive infrastructure maintenance and improvement so that they are able to assure the
fulfillment of the proposed major objectives?
   Discussions on this question have not brought about a unanimously accepted
standpoint by experts in the field. The labor force percentage involved in the
respective activities is as variable as the sums necessary for accomplishing and
functioning development infrastructures, sums that may result in significant
80                                                                            A. Trifu


growths first as a consequence of ITC advancements and also through the prism of
population number and density growth. This situation also considers an optimum
expenditure of a level which must fall within the desirable beach, taking into
account social and economic dimensions and the potentials which the national
economy has available.
   Economic infrastructure’s permanent care and improvement (as shaped by this
author) represent a vital aspect so that civilization (nation) could continue its
temporal existence and at the same time hinder its collapse. Those who had in
view this problem permanently assured for advance (constituting themselves into
civilizations “core”) to others’ detriment, i.e., so-called periphery, according to
Immanuel Wallerstein’s theory.1
   An impulse which is necessary for developing an emerging economy’s infra-
structure was given in the 16th mid-century Florence, which Wallerstein considers
as the moment of new European space creation, under the form of a “world
economy”. Similarly, the world modern system, that is, in both North and West
Europe’s structure, Western civilization was born and it rapidly developed the
world over, that market, urban society, as sociologists termed it, with decisive
impact upon the entire development of states and communities throughout the
world.
   From the core of economic infrastructure, the determining element in
accomplishing the above-mentioned desires is transport infrastructure, taking
into account that transport and shipping are considered the circulatory system of
any economy, the element which sets in motion and therefore in connection with
various areas (regions) of a country, but especially neighboring countries decisively
contributing to creating development regions, also assuring at the same time
numerous relationship between the world’s states. Upon the structure of this
infrastructure category, other functional components of economic infrastructure
can also be built and developed.
   We are in full action and affirmation of globalization, a phenomenon which
implies among other characteristics strong interaction between the world’s states
through increasing unfolding trade operations unfolding velocity that results, per
total, in general growth of economic efficiency through gathering and accentuating
the resource and capital movement to more profitable areas or fields.
   With regard to this phenomenon inoculated to all economic and social life
segments and focusing on analysis especially upon transport infrastructure, three
key features crystallize in order to meet globalization requirements, features which
embody considerations and applications belonging to both present-day revolution-
ary technology domain and, especially, institutional domain of public force
involvement with proper creation of the respective infrastructures:
1. Interoperability, implying the means to find ways for the most adequate and
   flexible means and ways to assure the fluency of material, human and financial

   flows, and efficient utilization of integrated organization networks, not only within
   trans-border relationships but also within already formed economic regions.
2. Interconnecting, which is a natural consequence of the former and claims
   especially technical order aspects to assure compatibility and quality in func-
   tioning transport and communication between neighboring states, and also in
   providing the entire network of service performance at most modern standards.
3. Considering intermodal flows and processes, assuring aspects which use several

   costs be diminished and respective operation efficiency be increased through
   possibility of carrying out “door-to-door” action directly to beneficiary, taking

   The whole thinking of constructing integrated economic infrastructures
should be done not only in the short run, but also in medium and long ones so
that they assure productive processes and human resources in achieving economic
growth, as shown, and become an aspect closely related to economic development
general process.



5.3         Discussion

The most important conclusion coming out from this chapter so far is that economic
infrastructure development degree decidedly depends on a country’s economic
type and its general economic development level. Thus, an inefficient unviable
infrastructure asymmetrically developed, and it also stays at a low accomplish-
ment and sustaining level, representing the characteristics of a survival economy of
states within the category of those world’s less developed countries or most
indebted ones. At the same time, it is also necessary to take a closer look at
Abraham Maslow’s known concept, the Pyramid of human needs, which placed
on the first level, the lowest one, just the assurance of those necessary to meeting
primary survival needs. Not accomplishing this desire might become very hard to
imagine the chances of accomplishing the other infrastructural levels, especially
scientific research and innovation.
   Once all these elements have been accomplished, the economic infrastructure
would become more conducive for progress to be made in terms of economic
development. We are in a situation similar to that described by Lawrence P. King
and Balasz Varady in their 2002 article,2 in the sense that globalization Manichae-
ism (good and evil dualism existing in certain proportions within a certain phenom-
enon or concept) for the less-developed or developing countries (in-transition ones
included) makes necessary foreign direct investments in a manner that is able to

    King and Varadi (2002)

evaluate a country’s general potential and assure (help) economic development it
needs.
   Thus, policies regarding infrastructures, coordinated and shared between
participants to international exchanges, become obligatory requirements of
integrated development of increasing policy, implementing liberty, and growing
development level also for world’s poorest countries, a possible thing in the context
of globalization and due to risk diffusion synergy and cost diminishing not
generated by activities comprised in economic infrastructures category.
   For the world’s first economy, irrespective of economic cycle, one half of
economic growth registered in the last half of the century was due just to utilized
human capital and, more precisely, to its productivity within economic activity; the
other half being the result of investing in productive and technological means. But
these achievements, too, are also people’s work with their physical and intellectual
capacities.
   Here, it is the way human factor is well evaluated, motivated, and trained in
order to assure as much new values as possible at the level of the entire national
economy. And it is considered, thus, a safe means of exit from whatever economic
and social depression, and an adequate dynamic growth rhythm of GDP for the
whole year, reaching 7.1% (percentage) points, the thing under a condition when
demand was compressed in the last quarter of the last year, with direct impact upon
most activity sectors. Construction and agriculture registered growths in the fourth
trimester, while more industry, and also financial and real-estate activities and only
services for companies registered diminished.
   The fact that Romanian economy’s structural scaffolding is weak, GDP’s rate
not expressing any boom of a healthy economy, and shortcomings in the face of
disturbing external factors determined analysts like Merrill Lynch (much
challenged by the present crisis) foresee for Romania in 2020 a GDP decrease
with 5%.3

   We can say that the “good” things are represented by a healthy and sustainable
economy, with adequate base of infrastructure for a normal GDP rate, and on the
other hand, “bad” things are represented not only by the trend to avoid taxes and
regulations, in a word the shadow economy, but also by the effects of the actual

   Therefore, the shadow economy and the fraud temptation are expected to go
