Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial
Liability                                                                                    8
Harpreet Kaur




8.1       Introduction

In an incident thought to be the first of its kind in India, partners of the auditing firm
PricewaterhouseCoopers were arrested for their alleged role in Satyam Computers’
scandal. A corporate fraud named as “India’s Enron” was committed by promoters
of the company which shocked India’s corporate world. Promoters got involved in
the manipulations of accounts and inflated revenues. The incidence stunned India’s
corporate world; the resulting consequence was that rattled investors and the
government were left to pick up the debris in order to salvage what was left of
Satyam Computer Services Ltd. after its founder and chairman admitted to com-
mitting the country’s biggest financial fraud.
   An organized crime was committed in which the magnitude of the forgery
indicated that it was difficult to believe that employees at leadership level were
not aware of the manipulations. Perhaps they did not know the actual amount
involved, but they must have certainly been aware of the happenings.
   Satyam Computer Services was incorporated as a Private Limited Company on
24th June 1987 for providing software development and consultancy services to
large corporations. The company was promoted by B. Ramalinga Raju (Chairman
and CEO) and B. Rama Raju. On 26th August 1991, the company was converted
into a Public Limited Company.
   Ramalinga Raju acquired large tracts of land through his family-owned property
firm, Maytas Properties and the public-listed Maytas Infrastructure. To raise the
cash to pay for the land, he used his stake in Satyam as collateral. Things began to
fall apart at the beginning of the fall in the value of real estate properties and global
stock markets problems of 2008. Creditors began to call in their loans as the Satyam
stock they held as collateral was falling. Ramalinga Raju had to juggle his stocks



H. Kaur
National Law University, Delhi, India

K. Çalıyurt and S.O. Idowu (eds.), Emerging Fraud,                                   115
DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-20826-3_8, # Springer-Verlag Berlin Heidelberg 2012
116                                                                          H. Kaur


and assets to meet the situation so he came up with the last ditch plan (Srinivas
2009).
   On 16th December 2008, he announced that the 20 years old Satyam Computer
Services would diversify into the real estate business by acquiring two business
firms run by Mr. Ramalinga Raju’s family, i.e., Maytas Infrastructure and Maytas
Properties. Satyam had proposed to acquire a 51% stake in Maytas Infrastructure, a
company listed on the BSE and 100% of privately held Maytas Properties.
However, when institutional investors expressed their outrage, he was forced to
withdraw the proposal. This was probably a way to siphon off cash from Satyam’s
books to meet the debt repayment and cash obligations that two Maytas Firms had
to meet (Ganesh 2009). The deal involved total cash outgoings of Rs. 7,840 crore
($1.6 billion US Dollars), of which about Rs. 7,400 crore would have accrued to the
family members. After this withdrawal, the share prices of Satyam fell by 30% to
Rs. 158 per share on the BSE from Rs. 544 per share, when the company was ruling
the markets with their 52-week high. There are credible reports that Ramalinga
Raju family now owns just about 5% of the stock. This is down from 8.61% after
institutions from which he had borrowed money sold shares pledged with them
(Ganesh 2009).
   After this episode, the question arose as to whether Ramalinga Raju should
continue as Chairman of the company when in India only in rare cases can
promoters get pushed out of the management of their companies (Ghosh 2008).
This, however, brought Satyam, Maytas Infra, and Maytas Properties under the
regulatory scrutiny of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI). In the
meantime, a lawsuit was filed against Satyam by a British firm, Upaid Services Ltd.,
an old client of Satyam in Collin County Texas District Court seeking depositions
by Satyam Chairman, CFO, and its global head of corporate governance (Times
Business 2008a, 2008b). World Bank also announced its ban on Satyam on Decem-
ber 23, 2008 for 8 years for any business with it over bribery and corruption
charges. Class suits were filed in USA against the company.
   The first sign of internal dissension among the members of Satyam’s board of
directors appeared when Dr. (Mrs.) Mangalam Srinivasan resigned on December
25, 2008 on the grounds of moral responsibility. She had been on the company’s
board since July, 1991 (Times Business 2008a, 2008b). As the board members of
Satyam did not protest against Satyam’s decision to acquire Maytas or its valuation,
the independent directors on Satyam’s board came under heavy criticisms, with the
company’s corporate governance being questioned. The company claimed that its
decision to diversify into real estate and acquire 100% of Maytas Properties and
51% of Maytas Infrastructure was strongly debated with the full board signing the
deal. It was observed that Satyam’s board acted in contravention of all norms of
corporate governance while deciding on Maytas takeover issue (Ramesh 2009).
After the Maytas deal failed the company proposed to buy back its shares from its
shareholders. In order to strengthen corporate governance in the company and
improve investors’ confidence, the company hired DSP Merrill Lynch to assist it
in this step. On 7th January 2009, Ramalinga Raju faxed a confessional and
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                          117


resignation letter to the board of directors of the company and SEBI in which he
brought the following facts to the knowledge of board of directors that:
    “1. The balance sheet carries as at 30th September 2008:
1. Inflated (nonexistent) cash and bank balances of Rs. 5,040 crore (as against
    Rs. 5,361 crore reflected in the books)
2. A nonexistent accrued interest of Rs. 376 crore
3. An understated liability of Rs. 1,230 crore on account of funds arranged by Raju
4. An overstated debtors position of Rs. 490 crore (as against Rs. 2,651 as reflected
    in the books)
    For the September Quarter, the company reported a revenue of Rs. 2,700 crore
and an operating margin of Rs. 649 crore (24% of revenues) as against the actual
revenues of Rs. 2,112 crore and an actual operating margin of Rs. 61 crore (3% of
revenues). This resulted in artificial cash and bank balances going up by Rs. 588
crore in Q2 alone. What started as a marginal gap between actual operating profit
and the one reflected in the books of accounts continued to grow over the number of
years. It attained unmanageable proportions as the size of the company’s operations
grew significantly. The difference in the real profits and the one reflected in the
books was further accentuated by the fact that the company had to carry additional
resources and assets to justify higher levels of operations, thereby significantly
increasing the cost. Every attempt made to eliminate the gap failed. As the
promoters held a small percentage of equity, the concern was that poor performance
would result in a takeover, thereby exposing the gap. It was like riding a tiger, not
knowing how to get off without being eaten! The aborted Maytas acquisition deal
was the last attempt to fill the fictitious assets with the real ones.”
    Ramalinga Raju wanted to bring it to the notice of the board that neither he nor
the MD (including their spouses) sold any shares in the last 8 years—excepting for a
small proportion declared and sold for philanthropic purposes, and that in the last
2 years a net amount of Rs. 1,230 crore was arranged to Satyam (not reflected in the
books of Satyam) to keep the operations going by resorting to pledging all kinds of
assurances. Significant dividend payments, acquisitions, and capital expenditure to
provide for growth did not help matters. Every attempt was made to keep the wheel
moving and to ensure prompt payments of salaries to the associates. The last straw
was the selling of most of the pledged shares by the lenders on account of margin
triggers, that neither he nor the MD took even one rupee/dollar from the company
and have not benefited in financial terms on account of inflated results, and that
none of the board members, past or present, had any knowledge of the situation in
which the company was placed. Even business leaders and senior executives were
unaware of the real situation as against the books of accounts. None of his or MD’s
immediate or extended family members had any idea about these issues.
    Raju also proposed some steps to tackle the current situation like formation of a
task force in the last few days to address the situation arising out of the failed
Maytas acquisition attempt, Merrill Lynch can be entrusted with the task of quickly
exploring some merger opportunities, and a “restatement of accounts” prepared by
the auditors in the light of the facts that had been placed before the board.
118                                                                             H. Kaur


    This letter of guilt and resignation sledge hammered India Inc., dumbfounded
regulators, pummeled Satyam’s stock, knocked the bottom out of the market, and
caused a long shadow over industry in general and the IT sector in particular. It also
raised disconcerting questions about corporate governance, the role of auditors, and
independent directors. Satyam is not some fly-by-night operator; it was listed on the
NYSE, ranked 185 in the Fortune 500 companies and the US Government was
among its clients, and employed about 53,000 employees. The hole that existed in
Satyam’s books was around Rs. 7,136 crore.
    There was intense speculation as to what triggered Ramalinga Raju’s confession.
It seemed that the beginnings of the end came when investors forced Ramalinga
Raju to reverse its Maytas moves. The question that arose was that could he be
entrusted on all that he had disclosed or was there more to what he had admitted?
His letter explained that revenues and profits for 7 years were overstated because
there was fear that poor performance could result in a takeover thus exposing the
gap. But despite the overstated balance sheets, Satyam’s performance never
matched any of its peers. In a bid to prevent a takeover, in early 2000, Satyam
merged four companies owned by Srinivas Raju, younger brother of Mr. Ramalinga
Raju. This helped in raising promoters’ holdings, but after a fallout with S. Raju, the
promoters’ holdings fell again around 2003 (Rajawat 2009). According to the letter,
the actual operating margin was Rs. 61 crore or 3% of revenue which was consid-
ered to be very low for any IT company and quite unbelievable by the experts.
There was also a violation of Section 372-A of the Companies Act, 1956 which
provides for an investment up to 60% of the paid-up share capital and free reserves
or up to 100% of free reserves, whichever is more, with the resolution of board of
directors only.
    It is also reported that company’s employees’ payrolls were inflated. About
13,000 fictitious accounts of employees were reported to exist.
    The present study has been conducted in order to analyze the background that led
to corporate loot.



8.2      Findings

The findings with respect to the role of directors, auditors, regulators, and stock
exchanges are discussed below:



8.2.1    Exit Strategy

Ramalinga Raju had a well-organized scheme for moving out from Satyam gradu-
ally. The move began gradually in 2001 with 25.6% promoters holding only 8.6%
of the company’s equity in 2008.
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                            119


8.2.2     Financial Logic

It was a very convenient financial logic to inflate false revenues and show false
profits, but it was actually very difficult to carry this out in the company’s books of
account. It could only have been possible with the help of several people, besides
the auditors of the firm. According to experts, besides the Hawala route, the only
way to inflate revenues was by showing either false clients with false receivables or
real clients with false receivables. If false receivables were to be shown from real
clients, then it would have involved employees in sales and delivery from other
circles. Most of Satyam employees, past and present, ruled out the possibility of the
latter as nobody wanted to admit that they were part of the fraud. The CFO
(Mr. Srinivas) confessed to the police that he just followed the orders of Ramalinga
Raju and did not manage the bank accounts or monitor the cash. According to
Ramalinga Raju, the receivables were false; the sundry debtors’ figures were also
false. Sundry debtors were reflected on the assets side of the balance sheet as soon
as these funds were received by the company. In Satyam’ case, the total sundry
debtors at the end of the financial year 2007–2008 were Rs. 2,223.41 crore,
compared to Rs. 1,650 crore in the same period in 2006–2007, indicating a jump
of Rs. 573 crores. At the end of the financial year 2005–2006, this figure was only
Rs. 1,122 crore. According to CFO, sundry debtors were only Rs. 490 crore as at
30th September 2008, while the books showed them to be Rs. 2,651 crore. This
clearly indicates that Rs. 2,161 crore shown on the books was false. In the balance
sheet for the year ending 2007–2008, debt worth Rs. 61.60 crore was shown as more
than 6 months old debt, whereas other debtors less than 6 months old were shown as
Rs. 2,161 crore. The normal accounting convention requires that the cash flow
position of a company should be shown in the time period to which debts are
incurred when recording the transaction in the ledger and this should be closely
monitored by the analysts and auditors. Thus, there was lack of cash flow in Satyam.


8.2.3     Fictitious Accounting

To create false revenues, it might be necessary to create fictitious customers and
fictitious services would invariably need to be rendered to them and in the end
fictitious receivables would come through them. These fictitious receivables would
eventually end up as sundry debtors. Debts which are not easy to recover have to be
audited by internal and external auditors. This was not done by Satyam. In this case,
fictitious receivables were created and transferred to sundry debtors. These ficti-
tious entries accumulated over time as there were no payments to settle them.
However, whether these debtors vanished from the books after a period of 6 months
or whether they were shown as bad debts, it was neither noticed nor commented
upon by the auditors of the company.
    Forgery and manipulations were conducted under many heads which were not
mentioned in the letter by Ramalinga Raju. The books had to be balanced as the
reserves and surplus on the liability side were forged. According to Ramalinga
120                                                                             H. Kaur


Raju, out of the total cash reserves of Rs. 5,312 crore on the book Rs. 5,040 crores
did not exist. That means that either these reserves never existed or else they were
siphoned off from Satyam. These dealings with respect to cash reserves could not
have been done without active connivance of bank officials. Criminal investigation
for financial misappropriation is now being carried out and the parliamentary
standing committee on finance is also investigating the fraud.



8.2.4    Negligence of the Market Regulator—SEBI

Satyam had earlier won an award for best corporate governance practices. Its report
on corporate governance and code of conduct as shown on company’s Website
were in accordance with clause 49 of the listing agreement. However, the magni-
tude of the fraud clearly indicated that nothing was actually complied with. Even
then the company obtained a certificate from the auditors regarding compliance of
conditions of corporate governance as stipulated in the clause with a rider that
“In our opinion and to the best of our information and according to the explanations
given to us, we certify that the company has complied with the conditions of
Corporate Governance as stipulated in the above mentioned Listing Agreement.
We state that such compliance is neither an assurance as to the future viability of the
company nor the efficiency or effectiveness with which the management has
conducted the affairs of the company.” The auditors have not stated anywhere
that they have verified the explanations given to them. This certificate has to be sent
to shareholders and stock exchanges along with the annual returns.
   Regarding the board structure, clause 49 of the listing agreement says that where
the chairman of the board is a non-executive director, at least one third (33%) of the
directors should be independent directors. SEBI came up with a modification that at
least half of the board of directors of a listed company should consist of independent
directors if the non-executive chairman of the company is related to the promoters
or persons occupying management position. Another modification provided that the
gap between resignation or removal of an independent director and appointment of
another independent director in his place shall not exceed 180 days. Disclosures of
relationships between directors should be made in the specified document to the
stock exchanges. The regulator also directed the companies to ensure that requisite
qualifications and experience for the independent director would enable him/her to
contribute effectively to the company in his/her capacity as an independent director,
though it was non-mandatory (The Hindu Business Line 2008).
   SEBI ordered an investigation on the basis of Ramalinga Raju’s letter revealing
fabrication in the statement of accounts of the company. Its investigation will be
into affairs relating to buying, selling, or dealing in Satyam’s shares and
ascertaining whether any provision of the SEBI Act or regulations have been
violated. SEBI has not been successful so far in checking real compliance of clause
49. It is necessary to have more preventive measures rather than punitive measures.
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                           121


Senior management of Satyam sold about 60L shares of the company in 2008 only.
SEBI is probing whether the sale of a sizeable chunk of shares by FIs in lieu of debts
a few days before the confession letter amounted to insider trading. If the suspicion
is confirmed, the proceeds of the sale can be recovered from them and could very
well be distributed among the thousands of small investors hit by the sharp drop in
the value of Satyam shares.
    Here it becomes necessary to examine the role played by the audit committee in
corporate governance. According to clause 49, all members of the audit committee
are required to be financially literate; at least one member shall have accounting and
related financial management expertise. Two thirds (67%) of members are required
to be independent members and the chairman should also be an independent
director. The audit committee has the power to investigate any activity within its
term of reference, can seek information from any employee, obtain outside legal or
professional advice, and may also secure attendance of outsiders with relevant
expertise. An audit committee meeting is usually briefed by the company’s internal
auditors and statutory auditors, besides the CEO and the CFO. There does not seem
to be any reason normally for the audit committee to question the basic data, such as
cash balances, the liability statement, inventory levels, and accounts receivable.
These are presumed to be verified by the auditors by virtue of a process of
reconciliation statements received from the contracting parties involved in the
transaction. Even the audit committee does not have a chance to go behind or
beyond the figures furnished it by statutory auditors, who are expected to follow all
the relevant rules and precautions and accounting standards. The management, the
chief executive officer, and the chief financial officer are expected to provide
accurate data. The audit committee, by its very nature, does not have the compe-
tence or knowledge to question the basic premise of statutory auditors who
believe that they are given accurate accounts by the management and staff
(Venkitaramanan 2009). It is necessary for the audit committee to be competent,
diligent, informed, probing, skeptical, and capable of making sound judgment
independently of management (Deloitte Audit Group 2009).
    Satyam had a policy for whistle-blowing which provided that “all directors,
officers and associates of the company are responsible to report in good faith,
questionable accounting/auditing matters, internal accounting controls, financial
reporting (accounting complaints), any violations, fraudulent/unethical practices,
misconduct or such other genuine concerns, which are against the interests of the
company. It is the policy of the company to treat such complaints seriously and
expeditiously. The reporting, which will be free of retaliation and discrimination,
shall be in writing either by way of e-mail or letter. While the policy encourages
directors, officers and associates to disclose their names, the reporting may also be
made anonymously.” Even then no one came forward for whistle-blowing. Under
Clause 49 whistle-blower policy is a non-mandatory requirement. This requires
reconsideration in order to make it functional; probably access to independent
directors on the board should also be provided.
122                                                                             H. Kaur


8.2.5    Negligence of Stock Exchanges

It is necessary for stock exchanges to carefully scrutinize the returns filed with them
as part of the disclosure requirements. In regular quarterly disclosure of sharehold-
ing pattern of Maytas Infrastructure a distortion was found. An individual B. Rama
Raju was listed as holding 2.5% stake in the promoter’s category; the same name
appeared as holding 8.7% in the public category (Srinivas 2009). It is to be noted
that the SEBI has prescribed only the minimum limit for promoters’ shareholding,
and no maximum limit has been prescribed.


8.2.6    Negligence and Deliberate Omissions of Independent
         Directors

Directors owe fiduciary obligations, duty of care, diligence, skill, duty to attend
board meetings, duty not to delegate, and duty to disclose interest in companies in
which they are directors (Singh 2007). Duties are the same for both the executive as
well as non-executive (independent) directors. In this case, independent directors
have acted negligently in allowing the chairman of the company to take indepen-
dent decisions. The role of independent directors has been to act as a watchdog to
protect the interest of the shareholders who appointed them. As per clause 49 of the
listing agreement, independent directors shall periodically review legal compliance
reports prepared by the company as well as the steps taken by the company to cure
any taint. In case of proceedings against independent directors no defense is
permitted on the grounds that they were unaware of this duty. As independent
directors do not have any vested interests in the company, they are supposed to
examine, question, and take into consideration the protection of shareholders before
agreeing for any decision. Problem arises only when directors develop vested
interests in the company. Thus, it is necessary to have provisions in the Companies
Act to restrict reappointments of directors. Section 256 of the Indian Companies
Act, 1956 provides for deemed reappointment of directors. According to the Act, in
a public company only one third (33%) of directors are given permanent
appointments whereas the office of the rest of them is liable to determination by
rotation. The vacancies created by retiring directors should be filled up at the same
meeting. But the general meeting may resolve that the vacancies shall not be filled
up. If it has done neither, the meeting shall be deemed to have been adjourned for a
week. If at the reassembled meeting, neither a fresh appointment was made nor a
resolution was passed against the appointment, then the retiring director is deemed
to have been reappointed. According to the listing agreement, a person shall be
eligible for the office of the non-executive director so long as the term of office did
not exceed 9 years in three terms of 3 years each, running continuously. This
provision requires some consideration. According to the preface of the code of
conduct and ethics for board of directors and other associates of Satyam, “the
Directors (both executive and non executive directors) and Associates are respon-
sible to carry out their duties in an honest, fair, diligent and ethical manner. . .”
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                            123


The board of directors of Satyam had two executive directors, one whole time
director, and eight independent directors. But none of them seemed to have
followed the code of conduct meant for them. As per the definition of NYSE,
Krishna Palepu did not qualify as Satyam’s independent directors (Nag 2008). His
remuneration was Rs. 9,948,000 for the financial year ending 2006–2007, whereas
remunerations of other non-executive directors were within the bracket of
13–14 lakhs. They had received excessive remunerations. One should not forget
that duties of directors are intermittent in nature arising only at board meetings.
Listing agreement has provided a mechanism for the performance evaluation of
non-executive directors by a peer group comprising the entire board of directors,
excluding the director being evaluated. This review could be the mechanism to
determine whether to extend/continue the terms of appointment of non-executive
directors. The independent directors must have witnessed numerous indications of
questionable practices, but chose to ignore them to the detriment of Satyam
shareholders, employees, and business associates. An examination of the extent
of breach of trust and fiduciary responsibility of the directors and the board is
necessary to be conducted. They are bound to be held liable for deliberate omissions
and negligence. By claiming moral responsibility and resigning from the director-
ship, independent directors cannot save themselves from their liability.
   Under section 193 of the Companies Act, 1956, companies are required to record
minutes of all board meetings. In these minutes, the name of directors who
dissented or abstained from the decision and the fact that an interested director
did not participate in the discussion or vote are also recorded. Minutes of board
meetings are prima facie evidence of proceedings of board meetings. One can have
a fair idea about the role played by independent directors in Satyam’s case through
minutes if they are properly recorded and kept.
   Nair (1966) observed in a case, “. . .The deliberate falsification of the books year
after year could not have been the handiwork of the staff of the company; it could
have been at the specific instructions of those at the helm of affairs, and although
the rudder might have been in the hands of the managing director, it is their duty to
keep an eye on him and see that he steers a proper course.”



8.2.7     Negligence of Auditors

“An auditor’s duty is not confined him/herself merely because the task of verifying
the arithmetical accuracy of the balance sheet, but to inquire into substantial
accuracy, and to ascertain that it was properly drawn up, so as to contain a true
and correct representation of the state of the company’s affairs.” (Stirling 1887). In
the words of Romer (1924), “. . .auditor must be honest, i.e., he must not certify
what he does not believe to be true and he must take reasonable care and skill before
he believes that what he certifies is true. What is reasonable care in any particular
case must depend upon the circumstances of the case.” They owe their duty to the
company and company only. Every auditor has the right of access to books,
124                                                                              H. Kaur


accounts, and vouchers of the company. He may require from the officers of the
company any information he thinks necessary for the performance of his duties.
   Auditors for Satyam were Price Waterhouse. It was only at the beginning of the
current financial year that the Reserve Bank of India finally gave Price Waterhouse
the nod to audit banks again after a ban of 4 years after being banned for its role as
auditor of the now defunct Global Trust Bank.
   The firm’s Chief Relationship Partner S. Gopalakrishnan and Engagement
Leader Srinivas Talluri were suspended from taking on client-related work until
the completion of the investigation. Both were taken into police custody.
   After its investigation, the CID told the courts that S. Gopalakrishnan and
Srinivas Talluri were not part of PW but Lovelock and Levis (L&L), one of the
audit firms owned by PricewaterhouseCoopers International. The PW is said to
have put evidence that the two partners are the members of PW and not L&L
(Mohan 2009).
   Price Waterhouse had admitted that its audit report, which overlooked one of
India’s largest corporate frauds, is wrong as it was based on wrong financial
statements provided by the Satyam management. In a statement sent to BSE, to
the three new board members, and to the company secretary of Satyam, Price
Waterhouse tried to shift the blame of its inaccurate audit report on the false
statements given by the erstwhile software giant (India Business World 2009). If
this is true then the real value of such audit is very little for user of accounts
(Somanathan 2009).
   This confession does not absolve Price Waterhouse, the Indian audit arm of
PricewaterhouseCoopers, from allegedly failing to detect the falsification of
accounts as admitted by Satyam chairman.
   The Institute of Chartered Accountants in India (ICAI) has begun disciplinary
proceedings against the arrested partners of Price Waterhouse. If they are found
guilty, they could lose their permit to practice and face a penalty up to Rs. 5 lakhs.
As per a government directive, ICAI had also asked for the working papers or the
audit methodology that PW followed in the last 5 years while auditing Satyam’s
accounts.
   The questions that arise here are whether auditors were negligent in their duties,
were unaware of the real facts, or it was a deliberate omission. It appears that the
auditors simply believed the facts given to them by the directors and did not go into
verification of bank guarantees and books of accounts. The case highlights that even
the routine procedures and standards were not followed by the auditor. For exam-
ple, the inflated cash balance on books could have been easily verified. Conven-
tionally an auditor takes an independent, but neutral look at procedures, controls,
and records, based on a sampling of various transactions. Their primary aim is not
to uncover wrongdoings, but to satisfy lenders’ and investors’ interest on the overall
financial condition of the audited company. An auditor is not an investigator, but he
has to exercise diligence and skill in the discharge of his duties. The issue is that of
extent and depth of auditors’ effort in their exercise of due diligence. Auditors
should be restricted from putting excessive reliance on the statements given by the
management. Independence of auditors and their powerlessness in family-owned
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                             125


businesses have become a very important issue that should be taken care of by
ICAI. The need is felt that the ICAI should review the guidelines to audit firms.
Some proposals have been put forward that include rotation of auditors once in
3 years to restrict the association of a particular audit firm with a company for a long
period of time; a system of joint audit by two firms for listed companies should be
evolved.
   Internal audits of the companies are not mandatory. It is necessary for the
statutory auditor to mention if it is missing. However, companies must have an
internal audit department and hire chartered accountants for conducting internal
audit. There is a need to make internal audit mandatory. Statutory auditor should
undertake sample checking to know the effectiveness of the internal audit. In case
of suspicion he should conduct an in-depth checking of transactions. Suits for
compensation can be filed by both the company and shareholders against the
auditors of the company.


8.2.8     Investigation by the Company Law Board/RoC

Section 237(b) of the Companies Act, 1956 gives discretion to the Company Law
Board (CLB) to appoint inspectors on its own motion. The CLB is empowered by
the section to ask the government to act if there are circumstances suggesting fraud,
misfeasance or misconduct. “Section 234 of the Companies Act, 1956 confers on
the Registrar of companies the jurisdiction to call for information or explanation in
relation to any document submitted to him. A further power is conferred on
Registrar, who may after being authorized by a Presidency Magistrate or a First
Class Magistrate, enter any place, search or seize any document relating to the
company, or its managerial personnel, if he has reason to believe that it may be
destroyed or tampered with” (Hidayatullah 1966). The Registrar at Hyderabad has
reported that many of the required documents were missing from the office of
Satyam. On his recommendation the matter is now being investigated by Serious
Frauds Investigation Office (SFIO).



8.2.9     False and Inflated Pay Rolls

Satyam’s 53,000 headcount was inflated and millions of rupees were withdrawn
every month in the name of nonexistent workers. The question is whether
Ramalinga Raju was in a position to inflate Satyam’s headcount by 13,000 and
manage as many salary accounts, withdraw cash from them every month for several
years, and appraise these fictitious employees every year all by himself without any
support? Obviously it cannot be done single handedly. The role of finance and HR
head cannot be ruled out here. According to Pendse (2009), “We are talking about
20% of the total employee strength that did not exist. You can hide 10 people, not
13,000. There is obviously a team to manage this number.” There must have also
126                                                                             H. Kaur


been collusion with some employees of the banks where fictitious accounts were
maintained.



8.2.10 Loss of Shareholders

It is often said that the law provides little remedy to investors even when they suffer
due to corporate misfeasance. Shares are accepted as an inherently risk-prone
investment. But tortuous suits for negligence are possible at the behest of the
shareholders (similar to the class action suit already filed in the USA), who could
equally explore the option of collectively approaching the CLB for mismanagement
action. They can also file a case against the auditors as well as the company for their
loss. The question as to whether shareholders have the right to sue for reflective
loss in case of breach of fiduciary duty by directors and whether they could be
debarred from recovering loss under the rule against recovery of reflective losses
were an issue in a case. The court said that the rule against reflective loss was
not concerned with barring causes of action as such but with debarring recovery
of certain types of loss. The foundation of the rule against reflective loss was
the need to avoid double recovery. There was a powerful case for saying that it
should be applied in a case, where in its absence, both the beneficiary and the
company would be able to recover effectively the same damages from the
defaulting trustee or director. The filing of a claim for damages for breach of
fiduciary duty had the effect of barring the claim for reflective losses (BCLC
2004). Resignation by all the independent directors has also left the minority
shareholders of Satyam in the lurch. For protection of investments there was a
need to get bail out for the company.


8.2.11 Serious Fraud Office Investigation

On the recommendation of the Registrar of Companies in Hyderabad, who
inspected the books of Satyam and eight promoter companies to ascertain the
facts about the fraud, the Government of India asked the SFIO to probe the financial
irregularities in Satyam Computer Services and report its findings in 3 months.


8.2.12 Investigation by Central Bureau of Investigation

After investigation into Satyam “a unique accounting fraud,” CBI has enough
reason to believe that the scam involves a much bigger amount, close to
Rs. 10,000 crore, than what was disclosed. The Agency has retrieved over 7,000
fake invoices and forged documents showing fixed deposits and bank balances and
their evaluation shows that the size of the scam is over 9,600 crore. These inflated
figures were also reflected in the form of audit reports which helped the company to
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                          127


cheat the public who were purchasing its shares. Ramalinga Raju also gave false
and fabricated statements about high capital of the company. He forged documents
and created fake FD receipts to the tune of Rs. 3,300 crore. The FDRs were shown
as available deposits by the company. He also allegedly manipulated the bank
guarantees to show the balance in bank accounts as Rs. 1,800 crore. CBI is also
investigating the “digital evidence” about the share transactions at NSE and BSE
and has not ruled out the possibility of questioning some officials of SEBI. CBI was
probing the rotation of funds and role of front companies used in the rotation of
funds and has found that the accused had floated more than 320 companies and
nearly 60 companies had the same address.


8.3       Conclusions and Suggestions

If business friendliness is to be protected domestically as well as internationally,
corporate governance must top India Inc’s list of priorities. The biggest question
that has to be addressed immediately is that do we have the kind of business
environment and business culture that actually promotes good corporate gover-
nance and demonstrates it? The institutions that are responsible for integrity of the
system and the reliability of the financial information are required to go through a
rigorous self-examination. The market analysts, who consider themselves to be
more knowledgeable than the ordinary investors, failed to guess the pattern of what
was going on. It seems inconceivable that the company’s auditors did not know
what was going on. According to accounting experts, assets can be overstated, but
cash balances are difficult to manipulate without deliberate deception. The need of
the day is to have an advocacy perspective rather than an independent or neutral one
that auditors are required to take. It is said that auditors face a trade-off between
speed and accuracy. It is not necessary to give quick results but to give correct
results. There is a need to strengthen the internal audit process so that they can
become whistle-blowers if need arises. According to KPMG India Fraud Survey
Report, 2008, 42% of the respondents agreed that frauds are most likely to be
committed by senior management and the company’s board. The report has come
up with the conclusion that so far the internal auditors have been more successful in
the detection of frauds (Jain 2009a, 2009b). It is often argued that changes in the
syllabi of the ICAI have to go through a long process of governmental approval,
when most of these are technical matters that the ICAI should solely deal with. It is
the ICAI which has the wherewithal to understand what the professional issues
involved are and it is they who should be involved into it (Roy 2009). It seems to be
an argument given for the sake of argument only because government’s interfer-
ence is limited to constitution of ICAI only; mechanism for audit profession is
decided by ICAI only. There is a need to have strict penalty provisions for fraud
committed by auditors. There is a need to ensure rigorous follow-up and take
deterrent steps. ICAI has suggested certain changes in the regulation to get more
powers. ICAI currently cannot take action against any chartered accountant who
has indulged in professional misconduct or any other misdeed if he is not a
128                                                                            H. Kaur


registered member of the institute. Although ICAI conducts peer review of large
firms, conducts mandatory training programs for auditors, and monthly newsletters
point out ways in which companies might resort to “creative accounting” practices,
all these practices appear to be insufficient. It may come up with a guidance note for
its members on how they should keep a vigil on documentation provided by clients
for auditing (Agarwal 2009a, 2009b). ICAI should come up for constituting an
independent oversight board, which is an EU-mandated requirement. ICAI should
make a comparative analysis with the approach of American Accounting Associa-
tion (AAA) and adopt their better practices (Srinivas 2009). ICAI has decided to
equip professionals with state-of-the-art technology or forensic accounting, which
it thinks will be a potential weapon to detect and fight corporate frauds. It is
planning to set up a serious fraud detection cell to assist regulators like RBI and
SEBI to prevent such scams. Although appointment/reappointment of auditors is in
the hands of shareholders, it is actually the management which appoints convenient
auditors. The time has come to check this practice now. If the stand of the
profession is that a nonnegligent and proper corporate audit will not be able to
detect frauds of the size of Satyam, then the very rationale for a statutory audit is
lost (Somanathan 2009).
    Government of India had constituted Quality Review Board (QRB) for review of
audit processes in India. Head of QRB resigned during the period when questions
were being raised over the quality of auditing process in India (Doval 2009a,
2009b). This raises concern about the functioning of such high-powered
committees and their success in strengthening and improving the quality of the
processes for which they have been constituted.
    The SEBI was brought to fore as a market regulator in1992 and was given wide
powers to call for records and take action against erring listed companies. The lack
of effective enforcement and foresight in corporate regulation is evident from
frequent corporate frauds. For an effective investigation, it should be time-bound
investigation. The regulator needs to show that it can act without delay and act
tough. The performance of SEBI may face CAG audit for its failure to detect
irregularities in corporate governance. SEBI has been maintaining its funds running
into crores of rupees outside the government accounts (Thakur 2009).
    SEBI and other regulatory bodies have been ignoring the warnings given to them
about financial irregularities committed by Satyam for the last 6–7 years. According
to Sarma (2009), financial irregularities were found in Satyam in 2001 and an
inquiry was ordered in 2002, but there was no progress on the front. When World
Bank was planning to ban Satyam, information was sent to Prime Minister,
Nasscom, and IT minister to conduct an inquiry, but no action was taken (Shekhar
2009). In the aftermath of the Satyam case, SEBI has decided to introduce a peer
review mechanism to review the accounts prepared by a company’s statutory
auditor. In addition, SEBI has also decided to constitute a panel of auditors to
review the financial statement of all BSE Sensex and NSE Nifty companies. These
initiatives will add more layers to regulations, but the benefits of these may not be
significant (Som 2009).
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                            129


    There is probably a need to relook existing corporate governance principles. For
example, Canadian securities regulators have proposed an overhaul of the corporate
governance regime for Canadian issuers. The proposals feature a new policy that
articulates nine high-level corporate governance principles. Issuers will be required
to disclose the practices they use to achieve the objectives of each principle. This is
a significant change from the current requirement to “comply or explain” against
specific governance guidelines. An issuer should establish the respective roles and
responsibilities of the board and executive officers, the board should comprise
directors who will contribute to its effectiveness, a board should have processes
to examine its membership to ensure that directors, individually and collectively,
have the necessary competencies and other attributes, a board should have pro-
cesses to improve its performance and that of its committees, if any, and individual
directors, an issuer should actively promote ethical and responsible behavior and
decision making, an issuer should establish a sound system of oversight and
management of actual and potential conflicts of interest, an issuer should establish
a sound framework of risk oversight and management, an issuer should ensure that
compensation policies align with the best interests of the issuer, and the board
should endeavor to stay informed of shareholders’ views through the shareholder
meeting process as well as through ongoing dialogue. SEBI should introduce some
mechanism for examination of working of managing directors and for identifying
financial glitches.
    In 2008, Mumbai-based analysts at Clear Capital, a subsidiary of the Noble
Group, a global securities research firm, came out with a report titled “Creative
accounting threatens to rear its ugly head.” In their analysis of the financial aspects
of all the companies listed on the Bombay Stock Exchange’s BSE-500 index, the
analysts said that at least 60 companies were indulging in revenue manipulation,
mostly by recording revenues ahead of time and booking fictitious sales. The report
identified some 15 companies involved into cash manipulations through disburse-
ment of bulk of their loans and advances to companies in which directors have an
interest (Srinivas 2009). But it seems that no notice of this report was taken by the
stock exchanges.
    The role of the RBI has become crucial in bringing to book the bank officials
involved in the scam.
    High decibel shareholder activism has been found to be unprecedented in India.
The institutional investors in Satyam decided to act jointly to oust the company’s
management to protect their interests. This is probably the first time in India that
institutional investors have displayed such activism. In such situations, the
investors can either move to the CLB or call an extraordinary general meeting of
shareholders to change the management, including the board. Transparency is a
basic tenet of corporate governance, as with appropriate information shareholders
can exercise their rights and it is also a remedy for fraud and manipulation. As one
of the judges of the US Supreme Court Lewis Brandeis succinctly put it—sunlight
is the best disinfectant and electricity the best policeman (Som 2009). An example
can be taken from the UK regulatory system which focuses on shareholder empow-
erment. Under the new English Companies Act, 2006, shareholders are given a new
130                                                                              H. Kaur


statutory right to bring a claim, on behalf of the company, for negligence, default,
breach of duty, or breach of trust. This is wider than the current position in that
claims can be brought for negligence and because there is no need to show that the
person against whom the claim is made has benefited from his or her actions
(Deloitte Audit Group 2009). Comparatively, in India shareholders cannot file
such a claim against the directors; the company is required to be made pro forma
defendant.
    Pledging shares to raise funds is a common practice in India. Most institutional
lenders want assurance that the promoter borrower will repay the debt. This
assurance comes in the form of collateral such as shares and the lender and
borrower enter into a security agreement. This ensures that the collateral is collect-
ible if there is a default or is appropriately adjusted whenever the value of the shares
falls. In a falling market, lenders are forced to liquidate positions in accounts which
fall short of the minimum margin requirement (Roy 2008). The same circumstances
were faced by the lenders of Ramalinga Raju. Some preventive measure should be
brought to overcome such situation wherein promoters are restricted from pledging
their shares during the lock-in period. It is noted that transfer of shares by promoters
is not allowed during the lock-in period and pledge of shares is deemed transfer.
This fact must also be considered as the government has decided to review and
strengthen new Companies Bill, which is awaiting parliamentary approval to
prevent such frauds.
    SFIO is a multidisciplinary organization which has so far investigated the
irregularities in about 40 group companies. The Department of Company Affairs
carries out the prosecution for violations under the Companies Act, 1956; the SFIO
forwards investigated reports on violations under other Acts to the respective
agencies for prosecution and/or other appropriate action. The need is to look into
how much successful SFIO has been till now? It seems that the SFIO is yet waiting
for the government to arm it with ammunition in the form of personnel and
resources to carry out its role at a point when a lot of water has already flown
down the bridge (Law in Perspective 2009). The SFIO welcomes information on
alleged serious financial frauds from the public. Anybody having any complaint or
information in this regard may send the same to the SFIO in the prescribed
proforma. How many persons are aware of such a provision? It should be brought
to the knowledge of general public.
    Is Satyam fiasco a blessing in disguise as felt by many experts? Experts are of the
opinion that this episode might have exposed the underbelly of the Indian corporate
sector, but it will help in defining the role of auditors and fixing responsibility for
negligence or complicity. The scandal would help in plugging loopholes in auditing
processes and help make the system more vigilant (Doval 2009a, 2009b). The
question is should one wait for such a scam to happen and then strengthen its
vigilance or one should be proactive to invoke preventive measures?
    According to Ganguli (2009) (former Chairman of PricewaterhouseCoopers),
“Auditing history has shown that there is lesson to be learnt from every fraud.
Generally frauds come to light due to whistle blower or accidentally.” But can
8   Corporate Fraud: Auditors’ and Managerial Liability                                        131


auditors be allowed to forget that they are bound to act with due diligence and care
in the performance of their duties?
    It is important to remind ourselves that the existence of a democratic society is
based upon a fundamental principle of Rule of Law. This axiomatic principle has
been incumbent upon the functioning of all the three wings, namely the legislature,
executive, and judiciary. But it seems that all civil laws have become ineffective as
lame ducks to suit the convenience of the manipulators for cheating and violating
contractual terms. The episode clearly indicates that present laws and regulations can
easily be evaded. An important facet is that “justice must not only be done but must
also been seen to be done.” One can only hope that the wrongdoers would have to face
the demands of justice, and faith in the system is restored to a level where undesirable
operators would be deterred of the consequences of committing such a fraud with the
citizens of the country on the whole. Delivery of justice should be timely and actually
compensatory to the persons who have been affected by this fraud and not just a battle
won for the sake of academic discussion and statistical purposes.

The Effect of Corruption on Country-Level
Investment: The Case of Pakistan                                                          9
Raheel Gohar, Muhammad Shariq, and Fazli Azim




9.1      Introduction

Globalization and technological innovations create investment opportunities for
enterprises worldwide. Foreign direct investment (FDI) is one investment option
which firms choose when expanding into international markets. Firms pursuing
international business opportunities analyze a number of factors regarding the FDI
location decision (Dunning 1999). At the same time, countries compete to attract
foreign firm’s FDI inflows. One competing factor receiving increased attention in
international business is a country’s level of corruption. Media attention, academic
studies, and international agreements are increasingly focused on corruption. Trans-
parency international, a not-for-profit agency established in 1993, annually compares
country corruption and provides a corruption perceptions index (CPI) ranking to the
public through its website. The level of corruption for countries competing for FDI
inflows is readily available for potential foreign business investors to compare.
    What is the link between corruption and FDI inflows? Prior research has pointed to
both positive and negative influences of levels of corruption on levels of FDI inflows.
    The basic idea behind much of the analyses is that corruption represents a cost: it
increases the uncertainty associated with an investment opportunity, which in turn
increases the cost of capital and hurdle rates of return for an investment. All other
things remaining equal, this increase in the cost of investing will result in lower
levels of investment. At the same time corruption has been thought of as an
opportunity, where a firm’s managers can exploit weaknesses in a nation’s institu-
tional environment to gain advantages in competition.
    Before addressing the issue of the net effects of corruption, it is important to
recognize that corruption is just one dimension associated with the level of


R. Gohar
NUST Institute of Management Sciences, Islamabad, Pakistan
M. Shariq • F. Azim
NUST Business School, Islamabad, Pakistan

K. Çalıyurt and S.O. Idowu (eds.), Emerging Fraud,                                133
DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-20826-3_9, # Springer-Verlag Berlin Heidelberg 2012
134                                                                         R. Gohar et al.


transparency of a nation’s institutions. National institutional environments com-
prise the formal rules of a nation as defined by its political and legal institutions as
well as the informal rules, as they extend from the social and cultural dimensions of
a country. Corruption is a key aspect of the transparency of a nation’s political and
legal institutions and therefore has an impact on the level of political uncertainty
and risk and the burden of regulation as it is related to the extent of market
liberalization. Each of these dimensions in turn can influence the levels of FDI
inflows, yet each of these dimensions is not separate from one another.
    Indeed, corruption is often thought of as extending from, or being a consequence
of, other dimensions of a nation’s institutional environment. The role played by
the regulatory state in the economy has been generally acknowledged as one of the
major sources of corruption (Shleifer and Vishny 1999; Rose-Ackerman 1999).
The political and bureaucratic allocation of scarce resources through an elaborate
system of permits and licenses provides fertile ground for rent-seeking activities that
in turn favor the emergence and development of illicit behavior. If excessive regula-
tion stimulates corruption, then policy measures such as deregulation and privatization
might reduce levels of corruption, through reductions in opportunities for private rent
seeking by public officials (Rose-Ackermam 1988; Bliss and Di Tella 1997).
    Corruption, in this line of reasoning, emanates from the interaction of corporate
decision-makers on the one hand and politicians or regulators on the other. To the
extent that politicians/regulators are able to extract legal rents or illicit payoffs from
firms, this interaction has a direct bearing on the cost of capital and cash-flow
forecasts. Pursuing this chain of reasoning, the burden of the regulatory state
(of which corruption is a part) leads to a higher of cost of capital, a lower incentive
for firms to invest, and consequently a lower economic growth rate.
    In this study, we focus on the effects of corruption on investment decisions by
foreign firms that are on FDI. Although the intent of our study is not to investigate how
corruption levels can be reduced by market liberalization reforms (when those reforms
reduce the regulatory burden in an economy), these points do relate to the idea that
corruption is not only just one dimension of a national environment, it is a dimension
that could plausibly be a consequence of other dimensions. If this is the case, then it is
possible that any observed relationship between corruption and FDI inflows might be
spurious, or at least contingent on other dimensions of the environment. FDI inflows
could possibly be influenced by a dimension that is correlated with corruption levels,
or with a dimension that is thought to be a causal precursor to FDI inflows. We
investigate this point in this study, both conceptually and empirically.
    We construct this investigation by first reviewing the background literature on
corruption, domestic and foreign investment, and economic growth. We next move
to a discussion of corruption and its correlates in national institutional environments,
which is followed by our arguments about our expected relationships between corrup-
tion and FDI. We next move to an examination of these ideas using the empirical
context of FDI into Pakistan. From this, we conclude that the influence of corruption
on economic outcomes, such as FDI inflows, needs to be considered in the broader
context of a more full consideration of a nation’s institutions; otherwise the strength of
inferences one draws about the influence of corruption will be less conclusive.
9   The Effect of Corruption on Country-Level Investment: The Case of Pakistan      135


9.2       Literature Review

Mauro (1995) determined that there is a negative association between corruption and
investment as well as growth. A one-standard-deviation increase in the corruption
index will generate an increase in the investment rate of 2.9% of GDP. Thus, the
impact of corruption on investment is significant. Wei (2000) found that corruption
acts like a tax and its impact of FDI inflows can be measured as such. Comparing
corruption levels between Singapore and Mexico, Wei (2000) concludes that raising
the index of corruption from the Singapore level to the Mexican level is equivalent to
raising the marginal tax rate on enterprises by 50% points. Goldsmith (1999)
indicates that the new consensus view of corruption is that it stunts economic
growth. The Economist Intelligence Unit (2002) asserts that corruption’s damaging
impact cannot be underestimated and that there is clear evidence that it deters
investment.
   In a study on Swedish multinational firms by Katariina Hakkala, Pehr-Johan
Norb€ack, and Helena Svaleryd, these scholars using firm-level data on Swedish
multinational firms identified investments abroad driven by different motives and
found that the effects of corruption are not uniform. Given that a firm invests in a
country, corruption decreases so-called horizontal FDI and increases so-called
vertical FDI. However, corruption seems to have a negative effect on the probability
that a firm will conduct any investment in a country. This effect is smaller for larger
firms, indicating that the bargaining power of a firm may affect the cost of
corruption.
   Furthermore, in a research on Bureaucratic Corruption, MNEs and FDI by
Andreas Johnson, they develop a simple model that includes bureaucrats and
MNEs and describes how bureaucratic corruption in a host country gives rise to
increased MNE costs of operating. The model provides insight into the incentives
for engaging in corruption as well as suggestions of how to reduce the severity of
corruption. The conclusions of the model are complemented by an empirical
investigation that analyzes whether host-country corruption reduces the amount
of FDI inflows that the country receives. Preliminary regression results indicate a
negative relationship between host-country corruption and FDI inflows.


9.3       Defining Corruption

Transparency International (TI) has chosen a clear and focused definition of the
term: corruption is operationally defined as the misuse of entrusted power for
private gain. TI further differentiates between “according to rule” corruption and
“against the rule” corruption. Facilitation payments, where a bribe is paid to receive
preferential treatment for something that the bribe receiver is required to do by law,
constitute the former. The latter, on the other hand, is a bribe paid to obtain services
the bribe receiver is prohibited from providing.
   Understanding the multifaceted dimension of corruption is essential in order to
identify workable ways of dealing with it. Corruption comes in many guises. Bribery,
136                                                                      R. Gohar et al.


extortion, fraud, trafficking, embezzlement—but also nepotism and cronyism—are all
different manifestations of it. Even the most straightforward acts of bribery need not
always involve the exchange of money. Other gifts or advantages, such as membership
of an exclusive club or promises of scholarships for children, have been used as
“sweeteners” to clinch deals. Whatever form it takes, corruption is always a two-way
transaction; it requires a supply side (the briber) and a demand side (the one who
receives the bribe). That is why measures must be designed to hit both sides of the
corruption equation.
    Another aspect of corruption is that it can occur in many different sectors of the
economy. A commonly cited and morally reprehensible form is when government
officials abuse public trust by accepting bribes from private businesses. However,
the distinctions between the private and public sectors have been blurred by
privatization, and corruption within the private sector is not without pernicious
consequences as well. While not as common, bribes happen between public
officials too. And a recent bribery scandal involving government representatives
and officials of the International Olympic Committee in a bid to influence the choice
of venue for the Olympic Games is a reminder that propriety is something to be
maintained between public officials and respected nongovernmental organizations
as well.
    When corruption permeates a country’s political and economic institutions, it is
no longer a matter of a few dishonest individuals, but rather institutional, systemic
corruption. It is a phenomenon which thrives where institutions are weak or
nonexistent. And it is strongly related to poor governance. Systemic corruption
happens most where adequate legislative controls are lacking, where there is no
independent judiciary or oversight, and where independent professional media and
civil society agencies are absent. Reforms aimed at providing greater transparency
and accountability of public institutions and government operations are urgently
needed to redress such corruption.
    There is much to be done. And let it not be forgotten that wherever corruption
occurs and at whatever level, the ultimate victims of corruption are ordinary
citizens and society at large. That is why fighting corruption is so important.
Finding effective, credible, and enforceable measures to stamp out corruption and
to hold those guilty accountable is more than a noble objective. Our economic,
political, and legal institutions may depend on it.


9.4      Costs of Corruption

The cost of corruption is fourfold: political, economic, social, and environmental.
On the political front, corruption constitutes a major obstacle to democracy and the
rule of law. In a democratic system, offices and institutions lose their legitimacy
when they are misused for private advantage. Though this is harmful in the
established democracies, it is even more so in newly emerging ones. Accountable
political leadership cannot develop in a corrupt climate. Economically, corruption
leads to the depletion of national wealth. It is often responsible for the funneling of
9   The Effect of Corruption on Country-Level Investment: The Case of Pakistan       137


scarce public resources to uneconomic high-profile projects, such as dams, power
plants, pipelines, and refineries, at the expense of less spectacular but more neces-
sary infrastructure projects such as schools, hospitals, and roads, or the supply of
power and water to rural areas. Furthermore, it hinders the development of fair
market structures and distorts competition, thereby deterring investment. The effect
of corruption on the social fabric of society is the most damaging of all. It
undermines people’s trust in the political system, in its institutions, and its leader-
ship. Frustration and general apathy among a disillusioned public result in a weak
civil society. That in turn clears the way for despots as well as democratically
elected yet unscrupulous leaders to turn national assets into personal wealth.
    Demanding and paying bribes become the norm. Those unwilling to comply
often emigrate, leaving the country drained of its most able and most honest
citizens. Environmental degradation is yet another consequence of corrupt systems.
The lack of, or nonenforcement of, environmental regulations and legislation has
historically allowed the North to export its polluting industry to the South. At the
same time, careless exploitation of natural resources, from timber and minerals to
elephants, by both domestic and international agents has led to ravaged natural
environments. Environmentally devastating projects are given preference in
funding, because they are easy targets for siphoning off public money into private
pockets.


9.5       Quantifying Costs of Corruption

The short answer is “no.” Some experts use regression analyses and other empirical
methods in order to try to put a dollar figure on the cost of corruption. It is virtually
impossible, though, since payments of bribes are not publicly recorded. No one
knows exactly how much money is being “invested” in corrupt officials annually.
And bribes do not take only monetary form: favors, services, presents, and so on are
just as common. At most, one can research the correlation between the level of
corruption and, say, democratization, economic development, or environmental
degradation. The social costs of corruption are even less quantifiable. No one
knows how much the loss of an energetic entrepreneur or an acclaimed scientist
costs a country. Moreover, any estimated social costs in dollars would be inade-
quate to the task of measuring the human tragedy behind resignation, illiteracy, or
inadequate medical care. A general skepticism vis-à-vis any attempt at quantifying
the costs of corruption is thus warranted. The following example illustrates the
dilemma of pressing the issue into facts and figures.
    A power plant is being built somewhere in the world, at a cost of US$100 million.
It could be argued that—were it not for corruption—the cost could have been as low
as US$80 million. The financial damage to the public would then be US$20 million.
In practice, quite often projects are planned simply so that those involved can make
huge private profits. Assuming that the power plant was superfluous, the financial
damage would have to be assessed at US$100 million. Yet no major construction
project leaves the environment untouched. The results may be increased pollution,
138                                                                      R. Gohar et al.


a lowering of land prices, resettlement of local residents, an increased debt burden
for the country, etc. This calculation—probably closest to reality—is immensely
complex. On a global scale, it seems almost impossible. But even if one were able
to calculate the environmental damage, the increase of the debt burden, and other
factors, how would one measure the erosion of public confidence and the deteriora-
tion of a government’s legitimacy, which are the direct result of corruption?



9.5.1       Corruption Most Prevalent

At a first, indiscriminate glance, the CPI, published annually by transparency
International (TI), seems to confirm the stereotypical notion that corruption is
predominantly a problem of the South.1 While the Scandinavian countries come
out on top, most of sub-Saharan Africa ranks at the bottom. It would not only be
wrong to conclude, however, that—according to the CPI 2001—Bangladesh is the
most corrupt country in the world; it would also be counterproductive. The index is
not intended to brand any one country, or to pit the North against the South. Rather,
it is a tool to raise public awareness of the problem and promote better governance.
Corruption is as much a problem of the North as it is of the South. Recent scandals
in Germany, France, Japan, the USA, or the UK attest to that. It is well-established
checks and controls that make the difference in proportion. People are as corrupt as
the system allows them to be. It is where temptation meets permissiveness that
corruption takes root on a wide scale. Such an environment is more likely in the
emerging democracies of the South and East. There administration and political
institutions are still weak and pay scales are generally very low, tempting officials
to “supplement” their income. In dictatorial systems, meanwhile, administrative
and political institutions are nothing but an extension of the usurper’s corrupt
practices. The North also carries part of the responsibility for the situation in the
South due to its role as the bribe-payer. After all, it is largely Northern corporate
interests that supply the bribe payments. Until recently, governments of the North
not only tolerated these corrupt practices, but they even rewarded them with tax
deductibility. Fortunately, the 1999 OECD Anti-Bribery Convention has made the
bribing of foreign officials a criminal offence. TI has addressed this aspect with its
Bribe Payers Index (BPI), the logical complement to the CPI. In addition to the
question of the regional pervasiveness of corruption, the issue of corruption by
sector is also often raised. The BPI provides some statistical evidence as to which
business sectors are most prone to corruption. According to these results, the
problem of corruption is particularly prevalent in public works and construction,
followed by the arms and defense industry. The sector with the least detected
corruption was agriculture.

9.5.2       Global Corruption Barometer2

The Global Corruption Barometer 2007 explores experiences of households with petty
bribery. On average, more than one in ten of those interviewed had to pay a bribe in the
past year to obtain a service. The reported experience of bribery is very different for
people living in different places of the world; however, Fig. 9.1. provides information
on Transparency International’s Global Corruption Barometer.
   Countries most affected by petty bribery include Albania, Cameroon, FYR
Macedonia, Kosovo, Nigeria, Pakistan, Philippines, Romania, and Senegal.
Table 9.1 groups countries in quintiles based on reported bribery for services.


9.6         Methodology

9.6.1       Objectives of the Study

As mentioned in the previous section, corruption has a very strong negative
relationship with FDI in different foreign markets.
    This study was conducted to analyze effects of corruption on FDI in Pakistan.
Various other economic and political factors were also considered and their corre-
lation with FDI was studied. It is presumed that whereas corruption decreases FDI
in developed countries, it is the norm in developing countries and hence does not

have a significant impact on FDI in developing countries. It is written in manuals of
MNCs that they are to follow different standards while conducting business in
developing countries than what they follow in developed countries. For example,
there are many MNCs whose manuals contain instruction as to not accept bribes or
do bribing while conducting any of their business process, while the same MNCs
allow their African or subcontinent subsidiaries to be involved in bribe as
businesses are incomplete without it. This study was conducted to check corruption
effects in Pakistan’s FDI.
   Others factors included in this studied are different economic factors like GDP,
population, imports, exports, and inflation. Political factor was also included in this
study and measured by “Political Risk.”


9.6.2    Sample Size and Data

The sample size of the study is taken for 2 years, that is 2006 and 2007, making it a
cross-sectional study covering 2 years for Pakistan.
   Inflation remains the biggest threat to the economy, jumping to more than 9% in
2005 before easing to 7.9% in 2006. Furthermore, 2007 was a year marked by
instability and uncertainty for Pakistan.3 The FDI inter alia was constrained by a
number of factors namely, political instability, law and order, economic


3
  http://www.pu.edu.pk/economics/pesr/PDF-CurrentIssue/PESR%20Summer%202008/3%
20YOUSAF%20Economic%20Evaluation.pdf
9   The Effect of Corruption on Country-Level Investment: The Case of Pakistan    141


environment and no proper infrastructure, the instability in stock markets, and
regulatory regime. Nevertheless, FDI and foreign remittances provided a strong
base to improve the economic situation of the country.



9.6.3     Variables of the Study

This section will cover the variables involved in our study. The set of variables that
are relevant for the analysis are FDI, gross domestic product (GDP), exports,
imports, population, inflation, political instability index, and CPI.
   The FDI has been taken as the dependent variable and the rest of the variables
are all taken as independent.

9.6.3.1 Foreign Direct Investment
The FDI has been taken as the independent variable in our analysis. The values for
FDI have been taken from the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) for the years
2006 and 2007.

9.6.3.2 Gross Domestic Product
The GDP or gross domestic income (GDI) is one of the measures of national
income and output for a given country’s economy. A significant change in GDP,
whether up or down, usually has a significant effect on the FDI. We have taken the
values of GDP from secondary sources for our analysis.

9.6.3.3 Exports and Imports
Current theoretical studies have shown that international trade and investment are
complementary rather than substitutes if trade between two economies is based on
their comparative advantages. However, if the trade between the two countries is
based on their absolute advantages, there may be substitution between trade and
investment, as businesses decide to supply products and services through exports or
FDI. The closer the relationship between trade and investment, the better chance the
host country has of realizing the benefits from trade and investment liberalization in
terms of welfare improvement. If trade and investment are complementary, FDI
inflows are supposed to enhance the gains from trade.4 Thus, high level of exports
and imports in a country is likely to have a positive impact on FDI.

9.6.3.4 Population
Gross inflows of FDI are generally strongly and positively influenced by population
health in low- and middle-income countries.


4
  http://www.pu.edu.pk/economics/pesr/PDF-CurrentIssue/PESR%20Summer%202008/3%
20YOUSAF%20Economic%20Evaluation.pdf
142                                                                      R. Gohar et al.


9.6.3.5 Inflation
A high level of inflation, according to studies, drives away FDI. This relationship is
tested in our study.

9.6.3.6 Political Instability Index
FDI flows to transition economies unaffected by conflict and political instability
exceed those that would be expected for comparable countries that are affected by
such instabilities. Success with stabilization and reform increased the volume of
FDI inflows. The higher a country ranks on the political instability index, the lower
the amount of expected FDI.

9.6.3.7 Corruption Perceptions Index
CPI ranks the countries of the world according to “the degree to which corruption is
perceived to exist among public officials and politicians.” A higher scoring in the
range of 0–10 means more corruption exists in the country.


9.7      Methodology

Regression and correlation analysis were conducted in order to investigate the
relationship between dependent variable and independent variables.
   For this study, country size is measured as the log of population (logPOP)
(Delios and Henisz 2000). A country’s purchasing power is operationalized as
GDP per capita (GDPPOP) (Grosse and Trevino 1996; Kobrin 1976). Market
growth rate is measured as the annual growth in GDP (GDPGRW) (Wells and
Wint 2000). The combined export and import value as a percentage of GDP
(EXIM) is used for measuring a country’s international orientation. Inflation is
represented by the consumer price index (PRICEIND) of the country (Drabek and
Payne 1999). The political risk (POLRISK) index found in the PRS Yearbook is an
aggregate index of several dimensions deemed important for political instability.
The POLRISK variable is used in this study.


9.8      Results

The results of this study presented in Tables 9.2 and 9.3 show that FDI has a strong
positive correlation with GDP per capita, which shows that the higher the values for
GDP the more the inflow of FDI in the country. FDI is positively correlated to the
population. This shows that in Pakistan as population is increasing, the FDI also
increases as the market the MNC would cater to is large in this case and also more
labor would be available. Thus the large population attracts the FDI. Moreover, we
can say that health has a positive and significant effect on such inflows for low- and
middle-income countries. The gross inflows of FDI are strongly and positively
influenced by population health in low- and middle-income countries.
