    This study finds that FDI has a strong negative correlation with political risk,
which obviously implies that the higher the ranking of political instability in the
country, the lower the amount of FDI. Similarly a high inflation level makes
investment in Pakistan extremely unattractive for foreigners, which is why the
correlation is strongly negative. The balance of payments comprises the current
account, the capital account, and the financial account. The financial account is a
combination of FDI, portfolio investment, and other investments. The FDI can be
obtained by subtracting the increase in the domestic ownership of foreign assets
from the foreign ownership of domestic financial assets.
    CPI the best known of TI’s tools. It has been widely credited with putting TI and
the issue of corruption on the international policy agenda. The CPI ranks 180
countries by their perceived levels of corruption, as determined by expert
assessments and opinion surveys. The index scoring is from 0 to 10 with 10 being
the most corrupt. Pakistan ranks 7.92 on this index and has a strongly negative
relationship with FDI. This means that the more corrupt the country, the less the
inflow of FDI in the country.
144                                                                        R. Gohar et al.


  Conclusion
  This study was conducted to analyze effects of corruption on FDI in Pakistan.
  Various other economic and political factors were also considered and their
  correlation with FDI was studied. It is presumed that whereas corruption decreases
  FDI in developed countries, it is the norm in developing countries and hence does
  not have a significant impact on FDI in developing countries. It is written in
  manuals of multinational corporations (MNCs) that they are to follow different
  standards while conducting business in developing countries than what they
  follow in developed countries. For example, there are many MNCs whose
  manuals contain instruction as to not accept bribes or do bribing while conducting
  any of their business process, while the same MNCs allow their African or
  subcontinent subsidiaries to be involved in bribe as businesses are incomplete
  without it. This study was conducted to check corruption effects in Pakistan’s FDI.
      This paper has attempted to explore the effects of various factors like GDP,
  exports, imports, population, inflation, political instability index, and CPI on
  FDI. FDI has a strong positive correlation with GDP, and has a strong negative
  correlation with political risk. Similarly a high inflation level makes investment
  in Pakistan extremely unattractive for foreigners, which is why the correlation is
  strongly negative. The correlation analysis shows that FDI is positively
  correlated with exports and imports. Corruption does have a negative impact
  on FDI which has been proved through this study.
      Our analysis of the effects of corruption on FDI confirmed the outcome of
  previous studies based on older data. Corruption remains a significant problem for
  investment today. Given that the study included almost the entire world’s FDI
  inflow for a significant and recent period of time, the generalizability of the results
  is high. It is also suggested that public officials must realize that the macroeco-
  nomic environment and the institutional framework will continue to play a critical
  role in FDI decisions even with respect to (developing) countries being targeted
  for traditional market seeking motives. As part of their efforts to promote the
  economic space of a nation state (marketing a country), Pakistani government
  needs to know the relevance and importance of factors affecting its image.
  Corruption is one such factor. While it cannot be eradicated overnight, realistic
  targets need to be set. For policymakers, the impact of corruption on foreign and
  local investment can help determine the welfare loss and thus facilitate planning.
      Similarly, companies involved in international business are developing a
  position on corruption from an ethical, competitive, and risk-taking perspective.
  The preliminary part of such endeavors is to have an accurate idea of the impact
  of corruption.
      For future research, it is suggested that priority should be given to examine
  the effect of corruption at the company level. Are certain companies more
  amenable to corruption than others? And, if so, what specific characteristics
  differentiate them from the rest? Answering these questions will greatly benefit
  academics and practitioners alike. We hope that future research will further
  investigate the issues raised here.

10.1     Introduction

This chapter is organized as follows: first, definitions, concept, and the motivations
that lead fraudsters to execute fraudulent actions are clarified. Second, different
types of fraud in the banking industry are observed with emphasis on online/internet
banking fraud. In the third part, the detection and prevention of banking fraud
especially the methods used to prevent online banking fraud are stated and the pros
and cons of these methods are discussed.



10.2     The Fraud Concept in the Banking Industry

The Financial Times defines fraud as “a method of illegally getting money from a
person or organization, often using clever and complicated methods”. The Federal
Bureau of Investigation (FBI) describes the word fraud as “appropriation or illicit
usage of funds or goods entrusted to, in the custody or under the control of a person”.
   In general, Fraud is deception that includes the following elements:
– A representation
– About a material point
– Which is false
– And intentionally or recklessly so
– Which is believed




A. Y€uksel Mermod
Professor of Finance, Marmara University, Faculty of Economics and Administrative Sciences,
Department of Business Administration in English, Anadolu Hisarı Yerleşkesi, 34800 Beykoz/
Istanbul- TURKEY
e-mail: asli@asliyuksel.com

K. Çalıyurt and S.O. Idowu (eds.), Emerging Fraud,                                     149
DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-20826-3_10, # Springer-Verlag Berlin Heidelberg 2012
150                                                                         A. Y€
                                                                                uksel Mermod


– And acted upon by the victim
– To the victim’s damage (Albrecht et al. 2011)
   According to the definitions above, the act of fraud has the intention to deceive, lie,
or cheat in order to get some personal benefits. Fraud makes the fraudster to somehow
act in favor of his or her own best interest. The critical point here lies in the self-interest
mechanisms of human beings which enable the fraudster to rationalize the outcome of
the cheats they conveyed (Singleton et al. 2006). Banking is a highly information
based intermediary business that relies on information technology (IT) to access,
process, and provide necessary up-to-date information to all their customers. Banks
use the Internet technology as a strategic weapon to improve their market position in
their operations in a competitive environment (Agarwal 2009). Bank fraud is a crime
in many countries. The person attempting to intentionally deceive the financial
institution would plan the act in order to obtain money or some valuables from the
financial institution. The valuable or money could be in the form of banknotes, funds,
credits, assets, securities, or other items of value owned by, or under the custody or
control of the financial institution. In the widest sense, a fraud is a consciously
performed action that is supposed to benefit the executer but harm the victim psycho-
logically or in some other sense. The globalization of the world economy, modern
information and communication technology, and other factors make this crime a lot
easier to commit in the twenty-first century. Banking fraud occurs mostly because of
the lack of knowledge on the part of some citizens in different countries of the world
about the technicalities of the new banking activities, especially internet banking.



10.3      Some Facts About Fraudulent Actions

According to the periodic research made by the Association of Certified Fraud
Examiners (ACFE), the annual cost of fraud in the USA is nearly $640 billion. Most
of the fraudulent acts are carried by people in the accounting department (30% of
total frauds) and also by those in senior management (20% of total frauds). 42.1%
of fraud losses results with no chance of recovery while only 16.4% of the losses
can be recovered fully (ACFE 2006). According to a report prepared by KPMG,
regarding the profile of fraudsters and their activities, 85% of fraudulent people are
males, and 70% of total population involved in the crime is between the ages of 36
and 55. 68% of the cases are carried out by a single individual and 89% of the
perpetrators are company staff. Another dramatic fact is that senior management
represents 60.2% of the total fraud cases which reveals that the system of internal
control is weak, inefficient, and difficult to easily capture these fraudulent activities.
In only 13% of the cases the perpetrators are workers who have only been with the
organizations for a period of less than 2 years (Bozkurt 2002).
   According to one of the fraud theories criminality cannot happen without the
existence and support of other people. Learning criminal activities usually takes
place in some small intimate groups like families and friends (Bozkurt 2009).
10   Fraud in Modern Banking: Highlights on Online Internet Banking Fraud         151


A dysfunctional parent is more likely to raise a dysfunctional child. The learning
process of the crime consists of two parts: The methods to commit the crime and the
attitudes, motives, drives, and rationalizations of the criminal mind (Bologna 1996).
A dishonest employee can infect an honest employee in an organization and it can also
be vice versa (Sutherland 1949).
    According to some of the fraud auditing researches, it was found out that the
most common red flags for professional fraud are related with high personal debts,
belief that one’s job is in jeopardy, no separation of asset custodial procedures, and
no adequate background checking (Albrecht et al. 1984).
    It was also pointed out that the main reason for employees’ theft was workplace
conditions and the workers tended to underestimate the social costs of fraudulent
activities. It was concluded also that the real income of the employee was not
related to fraud (Hollinger and Clark 1983).
    The cruelty of financial markets comes from the reality that the participants who
construct these markets recognize only one value: money. An old proverb says
“money is the devil’s eye,” signifying the importance and power of money in
people’s lives. Money is power in the capitalist world and power means having
rights. Money is also the best incentive to make people work harder and it has a
magical power to have a control over them. When two people meet to discuss about
how to spend third parties’ money, the outcome is typically fraud and this kind of
discussion takes place typically in financial institutions, mainly in banks. The
temptation is ever growing between the market players. Investors seek for the
highest return, banks try to reach the best return on equity, and analysts and traders
try to gain the maximum premiums. In this very competitive environment, all these
participants are ready to do everything possible to reach their targets, even if
sometimes their ways might not sound very ethical. Financial markets rely on
shared ethical standards. Although people and institutions voluntarily limit their
actions, written rules and their enforcements are required in the system. People try
to avoid an illicit action if there is little chance of being caught; they try to
keep away from certain things if they think that it is unethical (Persaud and Plender
2007).
    The banking system is known to be one of the strictest regulated sectors. Good
quality auditing and internal observance of the norms make fraud difficult to
perpetrate, but only bank professionals who know how the system works and
have the knowledge to detect the potential weaknesses of the internal norms may
find some approaches to misuse the system. Fortunately, these people are usually
not involved in fraud issues as their education, background, and social and cultural
level limits them to be that much brave and unethical. But still in the past, we can
see some examples of some bank officials who used their specific knowledge of
bank management and auditing in order to maximize their own wealth or hide their
investment mistakes such as traders like Nick Leeson of the then Barings Brothers.
Leeson’s decisions had caused the collapse of the oldest English bank, reputable as
152                                                                     A. Y€
                                                                            uksel Mermod


the Queen’s bank which had financed the Napoleonic wars of centuries ago. In the
Barings’ case, the missing part and the gap were again the banking regulations; it
was possible at that period that the front and the back office of the bank could be
managed by the same executive person.
    After the scandalous Barings collapse this rule has changed, but what we
distinguish from the past experiences is: people are able to find some gaps in
the regulations and try to adapt their management using their exceptional knowl-
edge about the cracks in the system to use the system to work for their own good.
The leading motivation tools to make people work can be defined as the two “P”s:
Pain and Pleasure (Becker 1976). There is a reasonable equilibrium between pain
and pleasure in all our decisions. Depending on our needs we have the intention to
find a good reason to make ourselves right for our choices and we have the ability
to reason this choice according to our needs, virtues, and values (Dumbravă et al.
2008).
    In a capitalist economy some people are convinced that happiness and pleasure
can only be bought by money and banks are the excellent places for those money-
oriented groups to easily reach that kind of liquidity. Banks have been the target
location for robberies for many centuries as their traditional mission is to keep
safe other people’s money in large amounts in their safety deposit boxes Tierney
(2003). Now there are many forms of money such as credit cards, checks, security
papers, guarantee papers, and internet money which make the potential thieves
excited to set up new crime facilities.
    Banks are excellent places for those who would like to reach money as soon as
possible and with the minimum effort. The best way to be closer to the largest pool
of money is to be a staff and a part of that sparkling pool for those educated, sneaky,
and intelligent potential future burglars. We have to admit here that many bank
employees who were accused of fraud had no intention to be a part of such a crime
at the beginning. But after a while having the opportunity in the work environment,
the pressure and the incentives, the absolute trust of the directors, and logical
reasoning of that behavior could lead the innocent staff to listen to the devil instead
of their own consciousness.
    This phenomenon is called the fraud triangle (Fig. 10.1) (Cressey 1953).




                                                   Incentive/Pressure




                                                         Fraud
                                                         Risk


Fig. 10.1 The fraud triangle.
Source: Cressey (1953)              Opportunity                    Attitude/Rationalization
10    Fraud in Modern Banking: Highlights on Online Internet Banking Fraud                          153

     Trusted persons become trust violators when they conceive of themselves as having a
     financial problem that is non-shareable, are aware this problem can be secretly resolved by
     violation of the position of financial trust, and are able to apply to their own conduct in that
     situation verbalizations which enable them to adjust their conceptions on themselves as
     trusted persons with their conceptions of themselves as users of the entrusted funds or
     property.                                                                  (Cressey 1953, p 30)

    According to Cressey’s fraud triangle there are three motivators which are the
triggers for the fraudulent action:
    Incentive/Pressure: the source of pressure is either a financial need or a
nonfinancial need. One common financial pressure is gambling or drug addiction
problem; others can be the pressure of the expensive tastes and needs of a spouse or
lover or to impress the family. Sometimes the need can be more humanly like high
medical bills or high credit card debts. The motivation can also be nonfinancial.
There may be high pressure for good results at work or a need to cover up
someone’s poor performance.
    Rationalization: the person committing the fraud often rationalizes the crime he
has committed. Rationalizations may include, “I’ll pay that amount of money
back”, “They will never miss the funds,” “They don’t pay me enough, I deserve
more salary.” Or as rouge traders: “I will cover my loss soon and nobody will notice
my failure.”
    Opportunity: the person committing the fraud sees an internal control weakness
and, believing no one will notice if funds were taken, begins the fraud with a small
amount of money. If no one notices, the amount will usually grow larger. In that
case the cracks in the laws, regulations, and their restructuring are very important.
    Banking regulations are strict and complicated but not always sufficient to
regulate the whole financial system. No internal control system is infallible.
No audit or auditor can detect all system flaws. The smarter the people, the better
the regulations must be restructured and updated. Unfortunately to restructure these
regulations, people or victims almost always need a trouble like a serious financial
crisis or a financial fraud case. Unfortunately, dirty business and shocking
bankruptcies in the financial sector are often discussed in public with delays, after
their occurrence, when the huge losses shock all the performing “actors” at the fall
of the curtains (Voicu et al. 1998). Banking fraud is a big business in today’s world.
With more educational qualifications on the part of bank staff, banking is becoming
impersonal and increase in banking sector has given rise to white collar crime.
    Using the term fraud with regard to the banking sector one could say that it
mostly describes a process by which either a bank or its customers are defrauded of
money. Concerning the types of fraudulent acts it is important to understand that
there are different mediums through which the fraudulent action could be
committed. The most important ones are mail, phone, and the internet—which
covers computer crime and the resulting internet fraud which has developed into a
serious problem within the past decade as the use of the internet and online banking
has gained popularity in the global market. The most fashion crime in banks is
online banking fraud nowadays, which is the reason why we will highlight this
matter in this chapter.

10.4     Different Forms of Fraud in the Banking Sector

10.4.1 Internal Fraud

Rouge Traders: Rouge traders are bank insiders or managers who are usually in
very top executive positions and have the authorization to invest very large funds in
the name of the bank. The phenomenon that is described by the term rogue trader is
the following: a trader who secretly makes increasingly risky investments following
a very aggressive strategy by using the bank’s assets. As the risk of those
investments is high, the chances that the trader will suffer a loss from that invest-
ment decision are also very high. If that investment decision is not successful, rogue
traders engage into another more risky position to cover up the losses incurred from
the previous trade in order to generate a quick profit which would enable the trader
to cover the loss. These rogue traders are a serious threat to banks and there have
been some outstanding cases in the past in which some banks have had to close
down due to losses of several hundred millions because of such activities. Some of
the most well-known examples are John Rusnak, Nick Leeson, and Jerome Kerviel,
who defrauded Societe Generale of 4.9 billion € while engaging in derivative
operations (Jennings 2008).
   Rogue traders can be classified as internal fraud cases. First of all they have a
great deal of knowledge about their bank’s internal processes. The age group of
rogue traders can differ between 28 and 35. Concerning the first aspect we can state
that even though the control systems in present-day banks are very sophisticated no
system will ever be free from defects. Another critical point in the discussion of
rogue traders can be seen in the fact that they in some cases accomplish
skyrocketing performances. These seem to be too good to be true and most
superiors of the traders prefer to turn a blind eye to the problem of having such a
high risk after being strongly affected by their successful profit margins.
   Fraudulent loans and fraudulent documents: One of the main activities of a
bank is to grant loans, but this has to be made after a high-quality loan analysis and
the loan has to be given to creditworthy and trustworthy customers. Some
motivations and pressures on the bank staff for example having more bonuses or
meeting expected minimum branch performance per month cause the branch staff
to provide loans also to some people or companies who have bad credit ratings, or
those in immediate need of money for emergencies. A fraudulent loan is one that is
given by a dishonest bank officer or an accomplice. In some cases the “borrower”
declares bankruptcy or vanishes soon after the granting of the loan and the money is
gone. Some banks’ subsidiary companies during 2001 Turkish financial crisis took
some fraudulent bank loans from their own banks or their own special purposed
entities and declared bankruptcy afterward; at that time deposits in Turkish Banks
were all insured by the government, which meant that the victims were bailed out
by the Turkish government and consequently the Turkish taxpayers.
   Fraudulent documents, however, are mostly used in order to cover the theft of a
major amount of money. In this particular kind of fraudulent activity the fraudster
would fake some documents using wrong information in order to hide their credit
10   Fraud in Modern Banking: Highlights on Online Internet Banking Fraud           155


history or their income or making use of creative accounting techniques about his
financial position or identity and takes the loan concealing the fact that the money
has been stolen from this bank.
    Wire Fraud: This criminal act is done by using electronic communications and
wire transfer networks such as the SWIFT interbank fund transfer system. These
kinds of networks are used by banks to settle accounts and actualize overnight wire
transfers. There is generally the risk that insiders of the banks would use faked
documents that claim a depositor’s money to be wired to another bank usually to an
offshore account in one of the offshore centers which is quiet far away to control
instantly such as Cayman Islands or use the clients’ money for their own good and
pretend that the money is safe by replacing by another account through the wire
system when the actual owner of the emptied account appears.
    Uninsured Deposits or Banks: The risk of fraud is high when dealing with
unknown or uninsured institutions such as offshore or Internet banks which trade
without any license and have a professionally looking web page which is capable of
misleading potential customers into believing that it was safe to transact business or
disclose vital details, on the fake website. Depositors have to be alert about this
issue when choosing their internet banks.
    Identity Theft—Impersonation: Some fraudulent bank staff could steal per-
sonal details of the bank’s customer in order to steal their credit card or order a fake
credit card with the information of that customer for their own personal use or pass
it on to a fraudulent group for their own use. The fraudster does not necessarily have
to be a bank staff as personal information could be accessed very easily through the
internet as many customers foolishly display their information online. Impersona-
tion is the act of pretending to be someone else by using the data they could reach
and deceive the banks and their customers, if the fraud is made by an outsider that
can be recognized also as an external fraud.



10.4.2 External Fraud

Forgery made by using “checks”: Fraudsters can change the name and the amount
written on the surface of the checks (instead of $100, they can add one more “zero”
and ask for $1,000). They can also imitate the signature of another depositor or
create and publish one bank’s checkbooks and use the information of one of the
depositors.
   Stolen blank checkbooks are worth a lot to fraudsters who sign as if they were
the depositor.
   When someone illegally obtains credit from one of his bank accounts by paying
in a check from another account, taking money out against this check, and using it
for a few days before having to repay the original amount back is called check
kiting, it is another form of check fraud. Say you have a deposit of 500 in a bank,
and write a cheque on that amount and deposit it to your account in another bank;
you now have 1,000 until the cheque clears.
156                                                                   A. Y€
                                                                          uksel Mermod


    Booster checks are bad checks used to raise the amount of available credit of a
generally legal account or credit card. The amount stated on the faked check is
credited to the account as soon as the transaction is made even though the check has
not yet been cleared. This method is usually used to make a payment to a credit card
to increase the credit limits of that credit card.
    Accounting Fraud: Some companies use some fraudulent bookkeeping in order
to hide serious financial problems such as boosting the worth of the company’s
assets or profits or income when the company is operating at a loss and in need of
credits from a bank (Omurgonulsen and Omurgonulsen 2009). The losses are then
hidden using special accounting techniques. This means that auditors have unique
responsibilities to thoroughly control the connections and the payments made from
corporations to banks (Ilter 2009).
    Payment card fraud: This kind of fraud describes all forms of credit card fraud,
which can be characterized as stealing customers’ data and use their credit cards or
debit cards in order to illegally access their accounts.
    Duplication or skimming of card information: embraces copying the informa-
tion which is stored in credit cards as well as stealing the PIN code of the client. The
card will then be reproduced with special devices adding the information stored on
the initial card. In combination with the PIN code, which has also been copied, the
fraudsters withdraw money from the client’s deposits. The information stored on
the credit card as well as the PIN codes is copied as the thieves install certain
devices to the ATMs or is copied when customers pay their bill at a restaurant or a
shop. This action is the most common method used to carry out a payment card
fraud.
    Money laundering: Describes any scheme by which the true origin of the funds
is hidden or concealed and is an extended topic referred to in other chapters of this
book.



10.4.3 Online Banking Fraud

Convenience is the key reason of why millions of people are choosing to use
internet banking instead of traditional banking (Wallack 2009), (Singhal and
Padhamabhan 2008). Nearly 45% of the 141 million adults in USA pay their bills
online (according to the Garter 2004 Survey). Banks try to influence their clients to
use more online banking facilities as they can save money on operating costs.
However, during the popularization of online banking, nearly 2 million Americans
experienced fraudulent bank activity in 2004. Victims stated an average loss of
$1,200 per bank fraud. Most market researchers highlighted the increase in the
number of bank frauds in online banking.
   The term “online fraud” refers to any type of fraudulent method that makes use
of e-mail, websites, chat rooms, or message boards to present fraudulent actions to
prospective victims, to perform fraudulent transactions on financial institutions.
10   Fraud in Modern Banking: Highlights on Online Internet Banking Fraud         157


This crime’s most common way is to steal a person’s identity, and use it to his/her
advantage (Kellermann 2005). Most of the internet fraud events derive from two
steps: first, the victim’s account information, password, and username are stolen.
Second, the money of the victim is taken from the bank account by using the stolen
information. The online fraud schemes include but are not limited to:
    Trojans: A Trojan horse (or mostly used in a shortened form as Trojan) is a
tricky medium that comes out to execute an attractive function for the user, but
facilitates unauthorized access to the user’s computer system. The term is derived
from the Trojan horse story in Greek mythology. Trojan horses are designed to
allow a hacker remote access to a target computer system. Once a Trojan horse has
been installed on a target computer system, it is possible for a hacker to access it
remotely and carry out a variety of functions.
    Keyloggers: Keylogging is the practice of tracking the keys struck on a key-
board, typically in a covert manner so that the person using the keyboard is unaware
that their actions are being monitored.
    Keyloggers are small applications and mostly they are programmed by hackers
to gather information about the users. Old keyloggers were designed for personal
computers and laptops, but now there are many types of keyloggers for smart
phones and mini devices. Latest keyloggers not only record the keys struck, but
also capture the screen views and send them to a predetermined e-mail address.
These applications are installed in a few seconds, and mostly, the computer or
phone users would be unaware of the fact. Users can protect their systems with
spyware and updated antivirus software. The files from the unknown users should
not be opened.
    Phishing: Phishing involves using a form of spam to fraudulently gain access to
people’s internet banking details. The term “phishing” indicates the use of spam
e-mails which appear to come from a bank; in this way, criminals can “fish” for
legitimate bank customer’s login information in case the deposit owners get caught
by their angles. The spam e-mails contain a website that is designed to match the
actual homepage of a bank. If the user has no suspicion and enters his data thinking
that it is his own original bank’s website, he would disclose his password and all his
personal details to the hackers’ online system. The defrauders can withdraw money
from the customer’s accounts after inserting the information of the depositor on the
webpage of the original bank.
    Social Engineering: Social engineers use telephones or other media tools to get
secret information like passwords or personal details from customers to use in order
to steal their bank accounts or use their credit cards. They introduce themselves as
an employee of a bank or another related company and sound like a credible source.
Phishing can also be added in a type social engineering. Actually, people do not
want to give their information except someone promises to reward or punish them.
Social engineers try to attract people via gorgeous gifts or threaten them with the
perceived risk to security of their account to get the private information with
immediate effect.
158                                                                   A. Y€
                                                                          uksel Mermod


   Wi-Fi Sniffing: Today, many people connect internet via their wireless modems,
but if the security level of the access point is weak, their neighbors could also access
their information and misuse it. An attacker who accesses a wireless network and
monitors all internet traffic through the router could make inquiries on every
website that you visit, e-mail that you send, or user name and password that you use.
   Bank fraud detection in online banking: In order to be able to verify satisfac-
tory measures to detect fraudulent activities one has to consider the different forms
of bank fraud (Whateley 2009).
   Considering the different types of bank fraud most of the fraudulent activities in
modern times could be observed using computer technology-related fields.
Recognizing fraud in computer-based transactions is very vital for banking security
as the major fraud operations are actualized in this area Fest (2009).
   Detection of software is an important business for banks as they are more or less
obliged to offer secure services. This means that banks which offer online facilities
to their customers have to expand their IT-divisions.
   In order to detect bank fraud on a technical level certain algorithms are designed
which scan the customers’ activities on each account. The early signals of fraud can
be recognized by banks (i.e., sudden movements of frozen accounts, reduced line of
credits, and bad history of written check payments).
   Fraud prevention: In order to be able to prevent fraudulent activities in banks
the bank staff play a critical role. Regarding this fact almost all banks offer special
trainings for their employees in order to enable them to prevent potential fraud
cases. By these particular trainings fraud can be prevented before it happens.
Furthermore, potential financial risks can be eliminated. The bank staff will also
be able to recognize particular types of fraud and identify potential executors and
deny taking any further actions with them.
   Besides the measures described above, regulatory compliance is another mea-
sure taken to fight against bank fraud. Regulatory compliance describes the goal
that corporations or public agencies aspire to in their efforts to ensure that workers
are aware of and take steps to meet the terms of relevant laws and regulations.



10.5     Major Security Tools in Online Banking

10.5.1 Digital Signature

A digital signature is a mathematical format for representing the accuracy of a
digital message or document. A valid digital signature confirms to the recipient that
the message was sent from a recognized sender and has a trustable origin. Another
form of digital signature is mobile signature. The mobile signature is equivalent to
your own wet signature legally (Douglis 2009). Mobile signature is a new applica-
tion and one of the safest ways of using mobile banking. It consists of creating your
own PIN code by using your own mobile device and SIM card. Many banks have
started to use mobile signature. After January 1 2010, using a mobile signature or
10   Fraud in Modern Banking: Highlights on Online Internet Banking Fraud         159


“One-Time PIN” generation devices will be an obligation to access online banking
applications in Turkey.


10.5.2 One-Time Password

A one-time password (OTP) is a password that is only valid for a single login
session or transaction. One-Time Passwords can be generated by Electronical
tokens or cards or mobile applications. However, One-Time Passwords are weak
for phishing attacks. If the user types his/her username and one-time password in a
fake website, hackers can use his/her password for a while.


10.5.3 Personal Messages or Images for Homepages

One of the most efficient security tools against phishing attacks is personal
messages or images that the account owners can recognize immediately at the
homepage of their banks’ websites. Firstly, banks ask their customers to choose
an image or a message and then they publish this image or message in their
homepage. In case of a phishing attack, if the customer does not recognize his
predetermined image or message at the homepage, he/she will be able to find out the
fake site and report it. These security messages or images have very common usage
in online banking. But customers have to pay attention to remember their images
and messages.


10.5.4 Voice Authentication

Voice authentication is a capability to recognize a person by his or her spoken
voice. This is used for security purposes. Voice Authentication services are based
on “biometric voice authentication” by this way; the system has the ability to
recognize the customer’s voice, but unfortunately these systems are not used
worldwide (Gilhooly 2003).


10.5.5 Virtual Keyboards

Virtual keyboard is a software module that allows a user to enter characters via
mouse or track point. Many banks use virtual keyboard during login period against
keylogger applications. But latest keylogger applications also capture the screen
shots.
   All of these security tools are constructed to help the customer against the hacker
attacks, but the golden rule is “only use computers that you trust.” Publicly
accessible computers (such as those found in internet cafes) are not safe for online
banking.

                                                                                  uksel Mermod


   Conclusion
   Globalization and technological improvements have made many facilities easier
   in our lives. We do not need to spend so much time in banks’ queues to pay our
   electricity bills anymore. By using online banking we can easily do all our daily
   payments, ask for credits, or transfer money from one account to another as
   internet banking saves our valuable time and money. But the most important
   topic about the mobile banking applications is the security. Customers know the
   development about mobile banking, but they still have fear about its security and
   privacy and this is rightly so.
      The reason that banks and customers prefer online banking is because it is less
   costly and more convenient, but is it really safe? In this chapter after defining
   and explaining thoroughly bank fraud, highlighting the different types of bank
   fraud, we discussed the reasons and motivations behind fraud and fraudulent acts
   in the banking sector, security issues in banking, the potential banking fraud
   operations, and with particular emphasis on online banking fraud and made some
   recommendations on how to guide against this type of fraud.

11.1     Introduction

This chapter is organized as follows: First, the author discusses the role of marketing
in society by detailing some arguments about marketing’s conviction of misconducts
and the benefits the field offers consumers. Second, the author provides a definition of
fraud, corporate frauds (in terms of cases like Enron 2001 and WorldCom 2002), and
political frauds (such as the Nixon’s Watergate Scandal of 1974). Third, the author
delves into the issue of fraud in marketing, including multilevel marketing approach
and its relevance to fraud and mass marketing fraud. Fourth, the chapter explains
consumers’ vulnerability to fraud and fraudulent practices and consumers’ resistance
and boycott in protest of the incidence of fraud in marketing. Finally, the chapter
defines and acknowledges various consumer perpetrated frauds.
   What is Fraud in Marketing and Consumption?
   Sell them their dreams. Sell them what they longed for and almost despaired of having. Sell
   them hats by splashing sunlight across them. Sell them dreams–dreams of country clubs and
   proms and visions of what might happen if only. After all, people don’t buy things to have
   things. They buy things to work for them. They buy hope–hope of what your merchandize
   will do for them. Sell them this hope and you won’t have to worry about selling them goods.
                                                       (Helen Landon Cass in Twitchell 2000)

   Those were the words of a radio announcer, Helen Landon Cass, to a convention
of salesmen and published with no single clarification by The Philadelphia Retail
Ledger of June 6, 1923. What she said then could be understood in two different
fashions. First, as a “melancholy Marxist” you may read it as a hidden charge
of marketing and actually selling processes. “Does she think that consumers

164                                                                          € Y€
                                                                             U. uksel


are—dopes to be duped? What is she selling? Snake oil?” (Twitchell 2000, p 1).
Second, as an “unrepentant capitalist” you may read it as a call for associating
consumer products with their sold benefits and promises; that is, producers produce
goods which the consumers’ demand (Twitchell 2000, p 1). Well, a perfect match
with the definition of “marketing concept” in early marketing text books.
    Hence, an important question comes to mind: is all marketing adversarial and
fulfills a purpose in favor of the marketer? In fact, the relationship between
stakeholders (e.g., supplier, producer, seller, consumer, and buyer) may be
contradicting, in conflict, or cooperative and mutual, which leads to a win–win
situation as in the case of Relationship Marketing (RM). Nevertheless, unfairly in
many cases, the domain of marketing is mostly unjustly described as inherently evil
in popular literature. This unwarranted attribution to marketing by a popularist anti-
marketing standpoint is founded on arguments that marketing may convict one of
the following misconducts:
1. Marketing may harm personal autonomy in which the victim (i.e., potential
    consumer) holds only restricted self-determination
2. Invasion of consumer privacy
3. Humiliating consumer identities via stereotyping
4. Damaging competitors’ positions especially in saturated markets
5. Manipulating social values and virtues via the promotion of consumerism
6. Harming the environment, in which marketing applauds consumerism and
    wastes resources
    In particular, the effects of some of these misconducts have resulted in anti-
consumerism movements and attempts by consumers, termed consumer resistance
and boycotts, which we will discuss later in this chapter.
    Despite all these negative points argued by the popularist anti-marketing stance,
marketing offers many benefits for consumers. Hence, not all marketing activities
involve unethical issues or any fraud.


11.2     What Kind of Benefits Would Marketing Offer
         to Consumers?

Consumers are receiving more and more products that they really need or want to
obtain due to enhanced and proper use of market research. Consequently, the
number one benefit of marketing for consumers is that they are able to deliver
their voice, needs, wants, and complaints to the marketer. Accordingly, they are
able to shape what they want to get; that is, marketing gives consumers the power to
speak out and order their desires while market research allows marketers to hear
what their customers want. Marketing, especially the selling process and the
promotion process, allows consumers to be informed on what is available in the
market, what kind of needs the product satisfies, what the competition provides,
what the advantage of using a specific brand is, and so forth. With a lot of new data
on almost every communication channel, consumers are more able to know about
the product or service they need; hence, this marketing process assists consumers to
receive relevant and important information. Marketing gives them the opportunity
11   Fraud in Marketing and Consumption Practices                                   165


for better comparisons; this marketing is done mostly via promotional strategies.
Hence, the number two function of marketing is informing consumers, providing
first hand information. Consumers are increasingly subjected to marketing in a
multichannel way.
    Consumers search for, explore, and buy products online; get newsletters and
offers from their preferred brands via mail or e-mail; and use SMS text messaging
as they call and speak on the phone. So, in addition to receiving information by
being passively exposed to marketing activities consumers also have the choice of
being actively involved in receiving additional information via those channels.
Hence, the third benefit is the improved technique or system of receiving needed
assistance. Additionally, brand and marketing strategists provide consumers with a
promise reflected by the brand name; accordingly, consumers are able to shape their
expectations according to the brand they have chosen. Hence, managing brand and
marketing activities is vital and significant for selling promises for the marketers
and decoding brands allow consumers to shape or alter what to expect. Hence,
brands work as guidelines for the consumers on the quality that should be expected.
However, all these transactions or processes should be done in a fair manner by both
the marketer and the consumer, and should not cross the borderlines of ethics or
reasonable acceptance level of the public. Beyond some point of the acceptance
level, it goes into the domain of unethical marketing or unethical consumer
behavior, which also involves fraud in extreme cases.
    Marketing is blamed to be looking merely after the firm’s self-interest as does the
discipline of economics (Jones and Monieson 1990). Marketing acknowledges the
“ultimate power of consumer sovereignty” (Desmond and Crane 2004, p 1223). This
results in the well-known marketing concept within the discipline which argues that
the firm’s self-interest is only achieved via satisfied consumer needs and wants. That
is, marketing concept suggests that firms’ main goal is merely monetary and firm’s
care on their profit, but this profit is only obtained as an outcome of being and acting
favorably toward customers. In other words, customer satisfaction is an antecedent of
profits (i.e., firms’ self-interest). Hence, the outcome which the marketers long for
depends on the process of being favorable to customers and considering the morality
of marketing actions. Hence, unethical marketing or fraud in marketing cannot result
in productive monetary gains for the corporation in the long run.


11.3     What Is Fraud?

11.3.1 Definition of Fraud

In its broadest sense, a fraud is an intentional hoax, deceit, fiddle, trickery, and
cheating, which is legally a crime and civil law violation, made intentionally for
personal benefit and/or to give harm to someone else. Fraud as a noun has two
meanings: one referring to the fraudulent act itself and another referring to the
person who is conducting the false or misleading (i.e., fraudulent) act. Hence, the
former one is a deliberate and mostly planned distortion of the truth for a gain and
the latter is the person who provides this misleading and false information.
166                                                                          € Y€
                                                                             U. uksel


Fraudulent conducts or discoveries that are fake and deceptive information or data
are found in different domains such as in personal life, in business life, in the
financial systems, in the governmental systems, in marketing, in consumption
practices, both by the marketer or by the consumer, in art, in science, and so forth.
   “Consumer fraud is an expansive term that includes a seemingly endless variety
of unfair and deceptive acts. It usually involves some form of trickery used to
achieve an unfair advantage in retail sale. In popular usage, many consider any
bad buying experience to be a consumer fraud” (Noorgard and Norrgard 1998, p 8).
However, fraud in consumption practices should take not only marketer’s fraud
into consideration but also consumer’s fraud. Because fraud is not exclusively
committed against consumers, one should also consider fraud committed by
consumers (Lee 2000).

11.3.1.1 Fraud in the Corporate World, Fraud in Business:
            Corporate Frauds
Fraud in business is the use of dishonesty and deception or untrue claims by hiding
or omitting the real facts or some part of the real facts which is done for an
unlawful, material benefit which results in harming the interests of others. Corpo-
rate fraud is conducted by large firms or organizations rather than individuals. Two
of the most infamous corporate frauds, for instance, include the collapse of Enron
2001 and WorldCom 2002 which made auditing practice come under scrutiny.
Arthur Anderson, the audit firm for both cases, has ignored the overstated profits
and financial profile and statements of both firms and put its approval, committing
one of the largest auditing frauds in the history corporate fraud.


11.3.2 Enron 2001

One of the largest frauds and audit failure in the business world history is the Enron
Scandal in 2001 which caused two American giant firms go into liquidation and
shut down: The Enron Corporation, which was an American energy giant based in
Texas, and Arthur Anderson, one of the big five audit and accountancy firms in the
world. The emergence of the fraud liquidated Enron and dissolved Arthur
Anderson. When Enron was in billions of dollars debts as a result of failed
businesses, its executives managed to hide this poor monetary condition of the
firm from the public by abusing accounting loopholes and poor financial reporting
systems. Enron’s executives including the Chief Executive Officer, Jeffrey Skilling,
and Chief Financial Officer, Andrew Fastow, gave the wrong impression about
Enron’s financial position and deceived Enron’s board of directors and audit
committee of high-risk accounting issues, and forced Arthur Anderson to close its
eyes to the issues. Hence, Arthur Anderson was also involved in this fraud by
overlooking the real financial circumstances of Enron, misleading the folks. As an
outcome of this misinformation (i.e., fraud), the shareholders lost almost US$11
billion as the stock price of Enron shares fell from US$90 per share to less than US
$1 in a year which led to federal investigations. When these investigations brought
11   Fraud in Marketing and Consumption Practices                                   167


the fraud to the surface, the outcomes of the deception were disastrous for the
frauds. Most of Enron’s executives were accused of various charges and sentenced
to a long period in prison. Arthur Anderson, which was Enron’s auditor, was also
found guilty, lost its clients, and had to dissolve.


11.3.3 WorldCom 2002

WorldCom which was formerly named MCI was a main international telecommu-
nications carrier. Similar to Enron, WorldCom also revealed inflated profits
misleading the stakeholders. Consequently, WorldCom filed for bankruptcy with
an amount of more than US$10 billion debt, making it the largest accounting fraud
in US corporate history. However, in 2004, WorldCom emerged from bankruptcy
with a new name, MCI. It was paradoxical that WorldCom, shedding a US$36
billion in debt, recovered from bankruptcy with an improved financial outcome:
a clean balance sheet. A year later, the newly formed MCI was acquired by and
became Verizon Telecom.
   In order to improve the confidence in financial reporting of public firms, new
rules, regulations, and legislations were passed in the USA, although this did not
help those shareholders who only received limited returns on what they had lost due
to that fraud (Premeaux 2009). The exposure of the Enron Scandal and other
financial frauds (Asthana et al. 2009; Nelson et al. 2008; Verschoor 2008) in
some American enterprises in 2001 resulted in corporate governance being the
key focal point of related international organizations and institutions (Lee et al.
2009b). Another outcome of the Enron Scandal is the US energy markets’ price
manipulations in energy markets becoming an animated political and economic
subject matter (Marthinsen and Gai 2010). Accordingly, more emphasis is given to
financial transparency in corporate governance, or to factors influencing a firm’s
performance (Lee 2000; Noorgard and Norrgard 1998).
   Fraud in businesses is mostly used to gain some unwarranted financial benefits
from the governments in various ways. Sometimes firms hide information regarding
their employment, that is, by the employment of illegal or undeclared labor by
hiding the legal, and sometimes about their earnings to pay less tax (i.e., tax fiddles)
or illegal export or import of supplies (Hjalager 2007).


11.4     Fraud in Politics

The political scene of nations is not at all free from fraudulent scandals, for instance
the Watergate Scandal in the USA in the 1970s which resulted in the resignation
of the then President, Richard Nixon, and indictment and conviction of his several
administration officials in 1974. Five men from the “Committee to Re-elect the
President” broke and entered into the Democratic National Committee headquarters
at the Watergate office complex in Washington, D.C. on June 17, 1972. Subse-
quently, FBI associated the men with a slush fund, a corrupted auxiliary monetary
168                                                                          € Y€
                                                                             U. uksel


account (i.e., a reserve fund to be used in favor of the elections). They were charged
with conspiracy, burglary, and violation of federal wiretapping laws.


11.5     Fraud in Marketing

Fraud in marketing is found within the framework of marketing ethics which refers
to the ethical practices and regulations in the area of marketing. As marketing holds
in key areas of the markets made up of marketers, consumers, and the products and
services available in the markets, the parties involved in a marketing fraud may be
twofold which may also be conceptualized as a stakeholder-oriented discourse:
1. Marketers. Specifically, this is fraud conducted by the manufacturers, producers
    of the products or services, wholesalers, retailers, other intermediaries,
    distributors, or sales agents who are selling the products to publics.
2. Consumers who mislead the marketers.
    From marketers’ fraud framework, that is, process-oriented discourse, fraud
conducts may occur in areas or procedures of
1. Production, provisions, supervision, research, or conduct of products and
    services
2. Pricing issues and strategies
3. From physical distribution to selection or conducts of distribution channel
    members
4. Promotions including personal selling, direct selling, infomercials, sales
    promotions, public relations, relations with media and governmental entities,
    publicities, advertising, and media
    In view of that, fraud in marketing may be found in three different discourses.
1. Value-oriented discourse in which there are fraudulent issues that breach some
    values or virtues such as honesty, autonomy, respect to independence, privacy,
    confidentiality, and transparency. This discourse is with relation to the outcome
    of fraud.
2. Stakeholder-oriented discourse in which fraudulent conducts infringe stakeholders’
    interests (i.e., everyone who will be affected by the action of the firm, such as
    shareholders, employees, consumers, competitors, public, governments, and so
    forth).
3. Process-oriented discourse in which fraudulent conducts occur in marketing
    practices including production, pricing, distribution, placement, and promotion
    of products and services.


11.5.1 Multilevel Marketing Approach

Additionally, there are some corporations that use a very controversial marketing
strategy, the Multilevel Marketing (MLM) approach. The MLM is very debatable
among scholars and practitioners in terms of its legitimacy, that is, whether it is an
ethical and legal marketing action or fraud. An example is Amway Corp. that built
11   Fraud in Marketing and Consumption Practices                                 169


its whole marketing system according to this pyramiding approach. MLM is a form
of direct selling, and is also called network marketing, referral marketing, pyramid
selling, or pyramiding. Firms that employ this marketing strategy pay the promoters
for their own sales plus for the sales done by the promoters introduced to the firm by
the original promoter. Accordingly, the sales distribution channel of the firm is
made through the main (initial) distributors (i.e., sales force) as well as the new
sales force introduced to the system by the initial sale force. Accordingly, the firm
compensates the initial sales force for the sales made by them and for the sales made
by the new sales force. Hence, MLM involves initial members getting commissions
from recruiting new members which is outlawed in most states in the USA as
“pyramiding” (Vander Nat and Keep 2002).



11.5.2 Mass Marketing Fraud

Mass marketing fraud is fraud committed via mass communication media such as
telephone, mail, and the Internet. Mass marketing fraud is aimed at individuals,
businesses, or staff (i.e., employees of businesses). A fraud attracts potential
victims with fake assurances of cash prizes, products, and services, mostly for up-
front fees, taxes, delivery expenses, duties, or donations. These false promises are
typically “too good to be true” and are unsolicited offers; that is, they could claim
that you have received or won something which you never ordered or participated in.
Usually, this kind of fraud uses high pressure sales techniques such as that requires
immediate action, payments in advance, or personal information on the phone or
email. According to Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) report, some common
fraud themes are foreign lottery and sweepstakes, Nigerian letter scams (in which you
are required to provide your bank account details), credit and loan scams, overpay-
ment scams, and charity scams (see, FBI Report http://www.fbi.gov/page2/august09/
massmarketing_081409.html).
   The phony sweepstakes or lottery scams are targeted not only at individuals but
also at businesses. Some common frauds targeted at businesses include one in
which they pretend that they are the usual office supplies provider and request for
payments from the firm without delivering the products ordered. Or they could ask
you to buy a nonexistent advertisement space in a nonexistent business directory.
Or your firm receives invoices for unordered or undelivered products or websites.
   Mass marketing fraud involves three general domains:
1. Materially false or misleading representations made knowingly or recklessly
2. Deceptive telemarketing
3. Deceptive prize notices
(a) False or misleading representations. This involves intentionally or carelessly
    making, or accepting the making of and disseminating, a false or misleading
    material matter to the public. Those false claims may be in relation to the
    standard, quality, value, grade, composition, style, model, or history of goods or
    services or make false claims about their characteristics or benefits.
170                                                                             € Y€
                                                                                U. uksel


(b) Deceptive telemarketing. Telemarketing is the attempt of selling of products or
    services over the phone. Deceptive telemarketing is about the making, or the
    allowing of the making, of materially false or misleading representations so as
    to endorse the supply of a product or service during person-to-person telephone
    calls.
(c) Deceptive notices of winning a prize. They give recipients (potential victims)
    the impression that they have won a prize or any other benefit. They usually
    require recipient to pay money in advance or incur a cost in order to obtain the
    prize or benefit. In fact, the recipient has not won a prize, in fact, he hasn’t even
    entered a contest, and there was not a drawing of prizes in the first place.


11.6     Consumer Vulnerability to Fraud

Scam literally means a trick used for a gain in slang language; that is, scam is a
fraudulent business scheme. A person (i.e., a scam victim) is scammed (i.e.,
cheated) by a swindler by means of a stratagem. Swindle is to defraud one of
money or property or obtaining something by fraudulent means and a swindler is
the one who practices fraud as a means of obtaining money or property. Scam or
swindle represents consumer frauds. Scams cause a massive negative charge on
consumers and society. Scams’ exact annual costs are over US$100 billion and
telephone scammers cost over US$40 billion alone in the United States. The
globalization efforts (Schachter 1996) and the rapid growth in the Internet practices
have all added to these dishonest practices. Through the use of the Internet, con
artists have entered an increasing market and are able to locate untapped consumer
base which they could not identify easily before the advent of the worldwide web
(www). Unfortunately, all the preventive steps taken to date have been mostly
unsuccessful.
   Consumers differ in their scamming vulnerability, such as elderly people,
teenagers, young people, children, and disabled people would be more vulnerable
to scamming conducts than others. There are various types of scams applying
different baits and techniques; hence, it is not possible to define a typical scam,
but the pattern is almost similar in all these diverse scamming activities. Hence, a
typical pattern or procedure of scamming would be as follows: First, scamming
practices commence with the identification of a potential victim. The identification
of a victim may be twofold: by the swindler deciding on a particular target or
placing an advertisement to which a potential victim may react (reply). Second, the
swindler starts with the assessment process of the victim. Accordingly, the swindler
evaluates the target and decides whether the potential victim is worth further
conduct. Then, the deception commences in which the con artists would initially
gain the victim’s trust and focuses the victims’ attention on particular basic rewards
such as money, prizes, miracle cures, health or wealth promises, and so forth. Prize
promotions scams, for instance, are the ones which claim that you have won a
particular price from another country, say a firm in Canada (Kurt 1996). Next, the
swindler convinces the victim that some money has to be paid beforehand so that
11   Fraud in Marketing and Consumption Practices                                  171


the promised product or service could be delivered with tricky justification by the
swindler of this up-front payment to be made by the victim. For example, in the case
of prizes, you are asked to pay up front for the delivery or custom duties. After that,
the victim transfers the money in order to receive the product, service, or the prize
s/he has won. Finally, the swindler disappears; needless to say, no prize or promised
products arrive (Langenderfer and Shimp 2001).


11.6.1 A Potential Outcome of Corporate Frauds: Fraudulent
       Practices, Consumer Resistance, and Boycotts

Fraudulent practices of firms in particular or consumerism in general have allowed
people to engage in consumer resistance practices. They take the forms of anti-
consumption movements or consumer boycotts (Yuksel and Mryteza 2009).
A variety of recent and novel ideas surfaced with a significant attention offered
by consumer researchers in the domain of consumer resistance and anti-consump-
tion. Hence, resistance studies in social research as well as anti-consumption
literature are enriched with an assortment of different perspectives (Sandikci
et al. 2005) in conceptualizing the consumer resistance and anti-consumption
phenomena. However, there is still much uncertainty about the conceptions of
anti-consumption and consumer resistance with regard to whether they are
indicating toward the same underlying principle of withholding consumption.
    Consumer resistance is the confrontation of people toward some type of con-
sumption, fraud in consumption, or consumerism in general. Consumer resistance
has two facets. As such, the first facet of resistance may be an active (or proactive)
behavior in which consumers may aggressively attack, confront, conflict, and battle
a particular product. On the other hand, the second facet represents a passive
behavior (i.e., reactive in the form of post-action) in which consumers defend
their position, oppose, or refuse to accept a particular product. Consumer resistance
resembles anti-consumption in particular, when resistance is considered in its
second, passive facet. Anti-consumption emerges via inaction with regard to
stopping consumption (i.e., not purchasing, not consuming, etc.), as does the second
facet of resistance. However, consumer resistance may differ from anti-consumption
with its first facet, because anti-consumption is a passive mode of action while
resistance may be both active and passive. A protest or walk against an idea, a
practice, a corporation, and so forth, for example, falls in the domain of active
resistance. Although anti-consumption behavior may also be termed as proactive
(internally driven) or reactive (externally driven); this mode is related to the origin
or source of anti-consumption trigger or call which causes an individual to stop
consuming rather than the type of an active behavior (i.e., attacking versus
defending). More specifically, resistance embraces also anti-consumption phenom-
ena for resistance (which may sometimes represent a mere attitude) is an antecedent
of anti-consumption behavior.
    Specifically, anti-consumption means the inaction of people toward consump-
tion promotions, appeals, and practices by not purchasing. This phenomenon may
172                                                                         € Y€
                                                                            U. uksel


be associated with opposing consumption in general (as a life style) or in particular
(e.g., a brand or product category; Gopaldas 2008). They are then called selectively
practiced anti-consumption (e.g., politically motivated brand avoidance such as
boycotting) and generally practiced anti-consumption (e.g., a lifestyle choice such
as voluntary simplification; Lee et al. 2009a). People’s rationale in not consuming
or stopping consuming may vary. Consumerism is conceptualized as personal
happiness with consumption and the purchase of material possessions while anti-
consumerism is the outcome of a sociopolitical movement, a call for consumer
resistance against consumerism.
   The politically motivated brand rejection as an emergent form of resistance
behavior is a form of boycotting in which people refuse to purchase and/or use a
brand (i.e., anti-consumption) due to its perceived association to a particular
political ideology that the consumer opposes (Sandikci and Ekici 2009). Boycotts,
as a mode of consumer resistance, usually activated by an ethical or political
motivation, serve as a method for consumers to communicate their displeasure of
a company’s product or corporate behavior (Yuksel and Mryteza 2009; Zack 1991).
Hence, a boycott represents an active mode in its action or inaction by withholding
consumption, and therefore, conceptualized as consumer resistance and movement.
   Boycott literature conceals two key domains: (1) consumer boycotts as marketing-
policy boycotts (Sen et al. 2001) and (2) corporate boycotts as corporate-practice-
related boycotts (Yuksel and Mryteza 2009). Previous boycott literature covers
areas such as types of boycott behavior (Kozinets and Handelman 1998),
motivations that underlie participation (John and Klein 2003; Klein et al. 2004;
Sen et al. 2001; Tyran and Engelmann 2005), and firm’s effective managerial and
strategic responses to boycott calls (Yuksel and Mryteza 2009). That is, “the
relative effectiveness of the various marketing strategies, in particular the public
relations strategies, that firms use to combat the likelihood of boycotts as a
politically motivated form of anti-consumption” (p 248).


11.7     Consumer Frauds

Fraud in consumption practices should take not only marketers’ fraud into account
but also consumers’ fraud for the reason that fraud is not exclusively committed
against consumers, but also by consumers (Lee 2000). In fact, there are consumers
misbehaving themselves in exchange settings and are unavoidably influenced
physically, financially, and/or psychologically. These misbehaviors infringe the
commonly permitted norms of conduct during consumption practices. Accordingly,
fraud conducted by consumers in order to gain personal benefits is termed consumer
fraud representing the dark, negative side of the consumer. For example, fraudulent,
illegitimate, or faked consumer complaints fall in this category. These are mostly
intentionally, preplanned, feigned complaints of (“in fact”) satisfied customers
about calculatingly and purposely fabricated issues, such as untrue product or
service failures in order to receive, for instance, reimbursements (Kowalski 1996;
Piron and Young 2000; Reynolds and Harris 2005). Other forms of consumer
11   Fraud in Marketing and Consumption Practices                               173


misbehavior include shoplifting, credit card and check fraud, vandalism (mostly
among teenagers), “financial fraud and physical or verbal abuse of other consumers
and of marketers’ employees” (Fullerton and Punj 2004, p 1239). Daspin (2000)
reports other types of fraudulent misbehavior conducted by consumers from
“sneaking into first class on an airplane and sitting there while traveling on a
coach ticket”, to “playing on a golf course without paying green fees by starting
at the third or fourth hole”, to “driving away without paying after filling-up with
gasoline at a pump” to “buying items such as a party dress or a power tool, for a
single use, and then returning them” (Fullerton and Punj 2004, p 1239) to abusive-
ness toward marketers’ employees, to tag-switching, and to bad check passing to
name a few. These misconducts may possess various dimensions including the
nature of the misbehavior, degree of disruption, and the reaction(s) by marketers,
employees, and other consumers. The targets of the consumer misconduct may be:
1. Marketer’s employees
2. Other consumers in the exchange setting
3. Marketer’s merchandise and services
4. Marketer’s financial assets
5. Marketer’s physical or electronic premises
    According to these targets, varieties of consumer misconducts include (Fullerton
and Punj 2004, p 1240).
1. Directed against marketers’ employees (Curtis 1971; Richins 1983; Schultz
    1978):
    • Verbal abuse of marketers’ employees
    • Physical abuse of marketers’ employees
    • Willful disobedience of rules
    • Bizarre behavior
2. Directed against marketer merchandise (Best and Luckenbill 1982; Cox et al.
    1990; Schultz 1978)
    • Shoplifting
    • Fraudulent returns
    • Switching/altering price tags
    • Abusive exploitation of advisory services provided by marketers
    • Theft from service institutions
    • Coupon misredemptions
    • Use of forged or stolen tickets
    • Copyright theft
3. Directed against other consumers
    • Jumping queues
    • Illegitimate use of express or “cash only” checkout lines
    • Hostile physical acts
    • Annoying to ominous behavior towards other consumers
    • Criminal behavior in exchange settings
4. Directed against marketer’s financial assets (Albrecht et al. 1982)
    • Defrauding retail cashiers
    • Failure to report billing errors favorable to consumer
174                                                                                € Y€
                                                                                   U. uksel


   • False or questionable claims of injury on marketer premises
   • Bad check passing
   • Credit card fraud
   • Loan fraud
   • Fraudulent assertions to avoid payment
   • Warranty frauds
   • Insurance fraud
   • Computer-based consumer crime
   • Rumor generation
   • Sabotage of marketer’s goods
5. Directed against marketer’s physical or electronic premises (Schultz 1978).
   • Destructive theft acts
   • Vandalism (i.e., damage, destruction)
   • Arson (i.e., fire-starting)
   • Database theft
   • Denial-of-service attacks on retail websites
   • Spreading computer viruses

   Conclusion
   This chapter has explained the fraud phenomenon in the domain of marketing
   and the consumption world.
      Marketers like their other professional colleagues have also been affected by
   this white-collar crime of over time. It should not be assumed that fraudulent acts
   are only perpetrated against marketers by consumers, but there are some not “fit
   for purpose” marketers who defame the profession by carrying out fraudulent
   acts against their consumers. They like individual fraudsters should similarly be
   made to pay the price of the socially irresponsible acts.
