            Part IV
Concluding Remarks
Emerging Fraud: The Summing Up
                                                                                    12
Samuel O. Idowu




12.1     Introduction

To say that the modern business environment is permeated with fraud and all its
related socially unacceptable criminal acts, which are perpetrated by the so-called
white-collar criminals, is neither an attempt to frighten anyone nor to overempha-
size the seriousness of the problem.
   Historically, the crime of fraud and deception is not new, but the incidence has
been alarmingly high in recent years. In recent years, the forms the crime is
perpetuated have changed drastically due to modern advancements. These crimes
are costing the global economy dearly in both financial and nonfinancial terms.
Several of the preceding chapters have quantified the financial costs to stakeholders
of some of the resulting consequences of fraud, corruption, and financial reporting
scandals in many countries. It appears that the nonfinancial consequences might be
very difficult to quantify, simply because the social impacts of the crimes could affect
an unquantifiable number of people and the environment. This, therefore, means that
stakeholders need more decisive actions from governments, corporate entities, law
enforcing agencies, and everyone regardless of where they live on planet Earth. The
problem is everybody’s business, and finding solutions to it must involve everyone!



12.2     Modern Technology and White-Collar Crimes

The advent of the Internet has in fact exacerbated the problem. Fraudsters in some
parts of the world are taking advantage of modern technology—the Internet, to
impersonate financial institutions or other corporate entities in order to retrieve
confidential personal details from unsuspecting innocent citizens either in their own


S.O. Idowu
Senior Lecturer in Management Accounting, London Metropolitan University, UK
e-mail: s.idowu@londonmet.ac.uk

K. Çalıyurt and S.O. Idowu (eds.), Emerging Fraud,                                 179
DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-20826-3_12, # Springer-Verlag Berlin Heidelberg 2012
180                                                                         S.O. Idowu


country of abode or in some foreign countries in order to commit crimes—this is
unsustainable. Some gullible individuals when told by these scammers through the
email or text messages on the cell phone that they have won a large sum of money
or some parcels are on the way to them but they need to provide some personal
details or pay some money upfront before the prize could be released to them
easily succumb to such unbelievable statements. They too are either fraudulent
individuals or certainly gullible and living in a dream world! Such individuals need
to be forewarned that anything too good to believe is definitely false.


12.3     Some of the Perspectives Taken by the Book

All the chapters in this book have competently addressed several of the pertinent
issues. In some cases, some of these chapters have either identified solutions to
some of the problems arising from the criminal acts or noted some preventive
measures which could be taken in order to avoid being taken for a ride by these
fraudsters.
   Mermod in a chapter on “Fraud in modern Banking: Highlights on on-line
internet Banking Fraud” provides a comprehensive list of some of the possible
scamming methods used by modern fraudsters to commit banking or financial
services-specific crimes. Mermod, for example, notes in the chapter that banking
fraud could be either internally or externally initiated and perpetrated by fraudsters.
Internet banking, we are told, has many advantages to both the bank and its
stakeholders. Should we allow criminals to deprive us of these advantages in
modern advancement? The answer from this author and of course a large number
of people worldwide is certainly a big no! This therefore makes Mermod’s contri-
bution a must read chapter by anyone still unfamiliar with all the online fraudulent
techniques used by Internet fraudsters. These fraudsters, as anyone who has seen
their scam would tell you, are indeed very skillful in making the information they
send to their victims look real.
   The issue of corporate governance has taken center stage in the twenty-first
century business management as a result of a series of corporate financial scandals
which have plagued both the developed and the developing parts of the world. This
is most welcome. Several of the chapters have understandably mentioned Enron
and of course the SOX Law of 2002 in the USA. Interestingly, Kaur brought to the
fore the case of what he describes as India’s Enron! The chapter provides a
comprehensive assessment of what went wrong in Satyam Plc, a former Fortune
500 company, and the roles the external auditors and some senior executives of the
company played in the total failure of corporate governance in the company. There
are certainly many lessons to learn from all these corporate financial scandal cases
highlighted in the book. The law should not be allowed to abandon innocent
stakeholders to suffer due to the fraudulent acts of corporate executives and other
third parties.
   Gohar et al. focus on corruption in several parts of the world with particular
reference to their own country of abode. Corruption is not a disease of any
12   Emerging Fraud: The Summing Up                                                181


particular economy; it is a general problem regardless of whether you are located in
the East, West, North, or South, but the seriousness of corruption differs from one
country to the next. The incidence of this unacceptable practice might be more
pronounced in the less developed parts of the world because of the level of poverty
in those countries which fall into this category. Corruption has many adverse effects
on economic and political developments. These scholars were able to examine the
effects of corruption on Foreign Direct Investments (FDIs) in Pakistan while
looking at the correlation between economic and political factors and FDI. Gohar
et al. in fact provide a league table of corrupt nations of the world.
    Çal{yurt who is very concerned about the incidence of fraud perpetrated against
SMEs believes that not enough has been done to protect small- and medium-sized
enterprises. This was perhaps her motivation in devising what she refers to as the
Fraud Free Company Model. Çal{yurt believes that any SME which uses this
model should be able to prevent fraud being perpetrated against it. The jury is out
on this!
    Forensic accounting is a growing field over the last few decades. Similarly, the
work of the Association of Fraud Examiners (ACFE) continues to increase in
popularity. The problem and incidence of fraud over the last few decades are
perhaps responsible for the growth in these two fields. Several of the chapters
have focused attention on both forensic accounting and the activities of the ACFE.
Sadiq in a chapter on forensic accounting argues that there is a correlation between
corrupt practices and life expectancy, and some of the roles forensic accountants
would play could extend lives. This perhaps connotes the impression that corrup-
tion is a health hazard, as a result of its social impacts. Without fully understanding
Sadiq’s argument, one might assume that the author was only making a mockery of
the effect of the problem. This certainly is not the case. Whatever public funds
corrupt politicians or others divert to the own private resources have social
consequences on citizens of the nation. For example, it prevents the building of
hospitals, buying the required drugs and medicines for hospital patients, the
employment doctors and nurses, etc. The absence of all these, needless to say,
shortens lives. The work of forensic accountants should hopefully provide the
warning signs that things are not in order.
    In conclusion, we believe that together we can all make a big difference in
solving these social crimes by our small actions in all our different areas in life. If
you asked a victim of fraud, he or she would tell you it is an unpleasant experience.
That man through his search for a better way of communicating with his fellowman
was able to discover and put to use the Internet should be celebrated, not abused by
some criminals! Greed is perhaps the root of corruption which encourages under-
development and poverty in the less developed world. Politicians and those with
easy access to public funds in all parts of the world should be aware of this.
